DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
June 4, 1937
On April 6, the Consul at Yokohama
reported that Japanese interests with
agents in California and in Panama had
been endeavoring to obtain Panamanian
registry for a Japanese vessel to engage
in fishing off the Pacific coast of
Panama. This despatoh from Yokohama
reports that application has been made
to the Panamanian Consul in Yokohama
for registration of the motor-ship AMANo,
owned by the Amano Fishery Company, Limited,
of Panama, for Panamanian registration.
There is no indication as to whether the
application has been approved.

I have the honor to refer to my despatch No. 509
AC/C
of April 2, 1937 regarding Japanese activities in the
RECORDING DESK
FILE - NHB
fishing industry of South China and the Philippines
and to report that such enterprises will undoubtedly
3o
be expanded by the formation of the Nippon Suisan Kaisha
(Japan Sea Products Company) which has taken over the
important fishing industry carried on at Hong Kong by
the Kyodo Gyogyo Kaisha, Limited. The Kyodo Gyogyo
Kaisha, Limited, took over the business of the Horai
Fishing Comp any mentioned in my despatch under reference.
The new concern, which represents the merger of
six Japanese fishing companies, is capitalized at Yen
100,O00,O00 accOrding to the SOUTH CHINA MORNING POST
of May 21, 1937, which comments as follows on the
activities of the newly formed comp any:
"Its operations, to include sealing
and whaling, have extended from the
Arctic Sea and Behring Straits to Antarctic
waters, where the company's Nissen Maru,
of over 10,000 tons and reputedly the
biggest whaling ship in the world, is
F/FG
already in service.
Westwards,
1

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.94)
-8 -
Westwards, the huge combine will take
in Formosan and South China waters, where
its predecessor has already to a very great
extent replaced the native indu stry carried
on by junks from Hong Kong and South China
ports.
Fish trawled in Hainan waters is being
sent as far south as Manila and other
Philippines markets.
In Hong Kong, the Japanese-caught fish
constitute 75 per cent. of the supplies
retail ed to c onsumers.
The company has at present seven speedy
motor trawlers operating from this colony."
The efforts of Chinese fis hing interests in Hong
Kong to recapture the business lost to the Kyodo Gyogyo
Kaisha will of course be made more difficult by this
consolidation of the large Japanese companies and it is
considered improbable in Hong kong that anything will
be accomplished in the way of breaking the strangle hold
whi ch Japan now has on the fishing industry of Hong Kong
and South China.
Respectfully yours.
Howard Donovan,
American Consul.

I have the honor to refer to my despatches No.
14.
295 of Apri1 6 and No. 302 of May 11, 1937, regard-
ing the motorship AMANO MARU, which Japanese interests
intend to use in deep-sea fishing off the coast of
9
Panama.
As stated in the first mentioned despatch the
Japanese intend to send two more of these boats, now
under construction, to Panama for the same purpose.
1937
I have to report that the Panamanian Consul, under
cabled instructions from the Treasury Department of
Panama recently issued Panamanian registry to the AMANO
MARU. Since doing so he has just received cabled ins-
tructions from the Panamanian Foreign Office not to
give Panamanian registry to any Japanese ships without
specific authorization of the Secretary for Foreign
Affairs.
F/FG

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
I have to enclose herewith a copy of the Alien
Crew List (Form 680, U.S. Dept. Labor) showing the
personnel of the AMANO MARU, which is sailing from
Shimizu for San Pedro, Calif. on June 15, 1937, and
probably arriving on July 15, 1937, after stopping
at Honolulu enroute. It is interesting to note that
this fishing ship, specifications of which were sup-
plied in the second despatch referred to above, has a
total personnel of sixteen persons, of which all but
four are officers. The Panamanian Consul states that
except the Master all the officers are members of the
Japanese Naval Reserve. The Master, a Danish subject,
will remain with the ship until such time as a larger
vessel being built by the same company is ready for
launching. The Master will then return to Japan to
take command of the new vessel. The Master stated
that the vessel would "do some fishing near Honolulu"
before proceeding to San Pedro.
Very respectfully yours,
Richard F. Boyce,
American Consul.

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
September 25, 1937.
JWD:
MMH:
PA/H:
A-S:
Mr. Sayre.
S:
Mr. Secretary.
The attached report is based on an
investigation of the Alaska Salmon Fishery
situation, with special reference to the prospects
of Japanese fishing in Bristol Bay. The
investigation was undertaken partly as a result
of tension on the Pacific Coast growing out of
the Bristol Bay situation, and, supplements
studies previously made of this situation by the
Department. There already has been vigorous
agitation on the Pacific in regard to the threat
of Japanese fishing in Bristol Bay, and anti-
Japanese incidents in future are not improbable.
For summary and conclusion of report only
pages 32 to 34, inclusive, need be read.
LDS/REK

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Proposed method of dealing with problem arising
out of the prospects of Japanese participation
in the Alaska salmon fisheries
894.62
The tension on the Pacifio Coast which has grown
ou of the, Bristol Bay fishery situation, and which
8
may result an anti-Japanese incidents and agitation,
EC
especially movements, such as have already been
threatened, for the stoppage of the handling of Japanese
ocin freight cargo at Pacifio Coast ports, renders it
2
of urgent importance that steps be taken as early as
possible to arrive at a solution of this problem. While
Alaska fisheries interests have, in a desire to cooperate
with the Department, voluntarily halted agitation on
the Pacific Coast, it cannot be expeoted that they will
continue to exercise restraint unless they are satis-
fied that the Department is doing its utmost to meet
the situation.
The desirability is suggested of thera being con-
stituted a Departmental committee to study the problem
E
41941
P/MFM
tions for governmental action with respect thereto. It
is proposed that the committee include at least one
representative

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
representative each from FE, Eu, Le, TD, EA and Ta,
as well as any other officers who might be able to
contribute to a solution of the problem.
It is contemplated that the committee would under-
take (1) a study of the data on the subject already
assembled in the Department, including the report which
has just been completed of a recent investigation made
by an officer of the Department of the situation on the
Pacific Coast and Alaska; (2) a review of action which
the Department has already taken or proposes to take to
deal with the situation with a view to determining
whether any change should be made in the Department's
approach to the problem; (3) to consider what further
research  should be made to place the Department
in position to evaluate intelligently all the factors
involved, and (4) to make recommendations to the ad-
ministrative officers of the Department upon action to
be taken to the end that this problem may be disposed
of as expeditiously and advantageously as possible.
(A number of individuals prominent in the Alaska salmon
fishing industry are expected to visit Washington during
the first week in October for conferences with Depart-
ment of Commerce officials upon fishery matters pertain-
ing especially to Alaska and the Pacifio Coast. Some of
those

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
those attending these conferences have informed Mr.
Sturgeon of their desire to be considered available
for consultation and discussion in regard to the
Bristol Bay fishing situation.)
FE:LDS:JWB:SMJ

"295
Yokohama, Japan, April 6, 1937.
Japanese attempts to extend Fishing
Activities to Panama
"I have the honor to report that Mr. Julio
Bricenio, Panamanian Consul at Yokohama, reports
that during the 1 ast few months Japanese inter-
ests, with agents in San Pedro, California, and
in Panama, have been tryin to arrenge for him to
license one or more newly built Japanese fishing
boats under Panamanian registry to engage in
fishing off the Pacific coast of Panama. It is
understood that one boat, called the AMANO, is
dompleted, and a Mr. Kristan Olbertson has ar-
rived in Japan to take the boet from Shimuzu,
Japan, to Panama via San Pedro, California. The
owner is believed to be engaged in the fishing
in dustry at San Pedro. The boat is to be equipped
with wireless apparatus. No other details regarding
the boat are available as yet. Mr. H. Shireishi has
represented the Japanese company in conversations
with the Panamanian Consul.
"Thus far Mr. Bricenio has hesitated to further
the efforts to register the boats under Panamanian
registry as he doubts the wisdom of permitting the
Japanese to operate in his country's fishing grounds
and thus to jeopardize the interests of Panamanian
fishing

Richare F. Boyce,

2. The Boaro is of the opinion, first, that a

regularly off the Pacific Coast of Panu a i the vicinity
of the Pacific Entrance to the Panana Cenal is undesirable
from a defense standpoint; second, that the development of
such a fleet sho ld be discouraged in every pos iile way;

boats under Panamanian registry to engare in fishing off
the Pacific Coast of Panama as proposed in the above letter

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
August 3, 1937.
The American Consul at Yokohama reported
on May 11, 1937, that application had been
made to the Panamanian Consul for Panamanian
registry for the motorship Amano, a vessel
built in Japan for the stated purpose of en-
gaging in fisheries. On June il, 1937, the
Consul reported that the Panamanian Consul,
under instructions from his Government, had
granted this application, but that subsequently
the Panamanian Consul received instructions
not to give Panamanian registry to any Japa-
nese ships without specific authorization from
the Panamanian Government.
The American Legation at Panama reported
on July 28, 1937, that copies of the despatches
mentioned from the Consul at Yokohama were
furnished by the Legation to the defence
authorities of the Panama Canal. The Local
Joint Board comprising the highest authori-
ties of the Canal Zone considered this matter
and reached the opinion that a fleet of fish-
ing vessels manned by Japanese and operating
regularly off the Pacific Coast of Panama
was undesirable from a defense standpoint;
that the development of such a fleet should
be discouraged; that the licensing of Japanese
fishing boats under Panamanian registry to
engage in fishing off the Pacific Coast of
Panama should be prevented; and that American
business firms in California might be contri-
buting to the maintenance of a Japanese-
manned fleet in Panama waters. The Board
decided

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
decided to present this matter to their
respective departments in Washington with
a view to asking the State Department (1)
to approach the Government of Panama to
establish a policy of not granting licenses
to foreign fishing vessels in waters adjacent
to Panama and (2) to advise whether American
registry for California fishing boats could
be restricted so as to require American crews
when operating off the Pacific Coast of Panama
The Amano Fishery Company, Limited, of
Panama is registered in Panama, the principal
shareholder being Yoshitaro Amano, who is
actually a resident and prominent Japanese
merchant of Panama.
There is also attached a confidential
letter from the Acting Secretary of the Navy
enclosing a copy of a report from the Comman-
dant, Fourteenth Naval District, in regard to
the Panamanian motorship Amano. It is note-
worthy that while the vessel Is of a gross
tonnage of only lll tons its crew consists
of 1l officers and five seamen. The American
Consul at Yokohama was informed by the
Panamanian Consul that all of the officers
are members of the Japanese Naval Reserve.
With reference to the opinion expressed
by the Local Joint Board that American busi-
ness firms in California may be contributing
to

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
to the maintenance of a Japanese-manned
fleet in Panama waters, it is noted from
the report of the Commandant, Fourteenth
Naval District, that the Amano will proceed
to the California Coast to the f ishing
grounds off San Clemente for the purpose of
obtaining a load of fish for the Van Camp
Sea Food Company.
With reference to the recommendations
of the Local Joint Board, it is suggested
that RA may wish to consider in conjunction
with FE what action might be appropriately
taken.

The Nippu Jiji, Honolulu, 1 July, 1937.
******
111 TON FISHING VESSEL BUILT IN JAPAN HERE TODAY
Calling here to replenish her fuel and water supply, the Amano Maru,
a lll-ton fishing vessel completed early last month at the Kanezashi
Shipyards of Shimizu, Shizuoka prefecture, Japan, at the cost of 125,000
yen arrived in Honolulu today at 5 a.m. and docked at Pier 26.
Making the voyage from Shinizu to Honolulu in 16 uays and 17 hours,
the Amano Maru will leave within 24 hours for Panama, where she vill be
employed in Tuna fishing by the Amano Suisan Kaisha, which was formed
last year by Yoshitaro Amano, Japanese businessman of Panama.
In comnand of the vessel is Captain A. Christian, a native of Den-
mark, and aboard are Hikokuma Shiraishi, who was appointed manager of the
fishing company last year after more than trenty years experience in the
fishing industry at San Pedro, and 13 expert Japanese fishermen whose ages
range from 26 to 37 years old and who are being taken to Panama as employees
of the new company.

ENCLOSURE (A)
ONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 42 === (confidence 0.99)
ANSLATION FROM THE NIPPU JIJI - July 1, 1937.
A Sign on Progress of "Marine Products Japan".
A Tuna Boat That Is Going To Be Active
On The Pacific Coast of Panama.
Amano Maru, The Pride of Japan's Product, Arrives
Here From Shimizu Harbor.
The Amano Maru, a new tuna fishing boat of the Amano Marine Prodcuts Company,
formed by the Japanese of Panama about a half year ago because they believed
that there is no place to advance to except the world's high seas nov. because
the doors of the world are closed to Japanese imnigrants, stopped over here on
its way to Panama to take on a supply of oil and water. She docked st Pier
26 at 5:00 A.M. this morning. She was flying the fleg of Panana on the stern
and the Company flag on the rear mast and was painted white when she cane in
here and made a big hit with the people sround the pier. She is scheduled to
sail within 24 hours after docking after taking on necessary supplies.
Will Exploit Treasure House Not Touched By Predecessors.
Yoshitaro Amano, who was born in Yokohama and who has been engaged in trading
business for over ten years in Panama, perceived that no one is engaged in
tuna fishing there though the Pacific Coast of Panama is a tuna fish treasure
house. The formation of the Amano Marine Products Company was planned early
last year and received official approval of the government about half a year
ago. The capital of the company is $100,O00 and Hikokuma Shiraishi who has
been in the fishing industry for over 20 years in San Pedro and who is an
authority in fishing has become its new manager.
Pure Japan Made Amano Maru Has Surprising Capabilities.
Simultaneously with the formulation of company formation plan in January of
last year, Shiraishi went to Japan and inspected the fishing industry there
and he not only got surprised with progressed fishing industry but nade a
decision to get the ship and men from Japan. The construction of Ameno Maru
was started in November of last year and completed early this June, and it is
the finest among the fishing boats. Its ~50 H.P. Diesel engine was made by
the iigata Iron Works and the ship was built by the Kanazashi Ship Builders
of Shimizu Harbor, Shizuoka Prefecture. Both the Iron Works and the Ship
Builders applied the best known modern ship building methods in constructing
this boat because it was going to be taken to a foreign country. The speed
of the boat is 10 knots; has a cruising radius of 30 days; not a vuiver from
the roughest wave; capable of hauling in easily O tons of fish & day; if taken
good care, it can be used for 15 years. Moreover, it has a wireless and other
complete line of equipment. In other words, it can be well boasted as a Japan-
made product. The weight of this boat is ill tons and cost 175,000 yen according
to the present price of iron, but since it was ordered before the iron price
rise, Shiraishi said, they gained 50,000 yen.

Finest Young Men Fron Various Fishing Villages.
The crew consists of Captain Christian Albertsen (a Dane with Panama citizen-
ship and who rose from a sailor on a sailing boat and who is a man of real
ability and a good friend of Manager Shiraishi for many years) and Manager
Shiraishi and 13 fishermen. Two of the fishermen are fron Nagasaki and the
rest are from various fishing villages of Shizuoka prefectures. They are
all the finest young men selected from various villages. They are all ex-
perts who have been going to the seas fron childhood. Their ages range from
26 to 37 and they are all in the best vorking ages and they are all anxious
to show the real value of marine products Japan in the waters of Panama. The
names of the whole crew are as follows:

maiden Voyage
This maiden voyage to Panama began at 2:00 P.M. on June 15 from shimizu
Harbor. The engine and everything worked fine & re ched Honolulu in 16
days and 17 hours. When it reaches Panama, it w engage in fishing in
distant waters with Panama as its center. The fis. cauht will be sent to
San Pedro fron Panama on a steamer and made into  ned fish.
There Is a Future In Havaii's Fish I. ustry
Manager Shiraishi said, "I held an interest in fishing in Hawaii fron long
ago so on our way here I made several studies and found tnat specie of fishes
are abundant and there is a lot of hope. I feel that the fishing boats of
the place like San Pedro will put an eye on Hawaii and will start coming out
this way in the future. I understand that live sa dine which is necessary
for tuna fishing lacks here and this point need to be studied. I believe
fishing in Hawaii has a good future so I intend to continue my research during
the trip out from here."
Moreover, Amano Maru ran out of live baits three days out from Hono-
lulu, and they used false baits and caught about 300 corphaena within a short
time and after keeping all that is necessary for food, threw beck the rest into
the ocean, v.e are told.

g from THE STAR & HERALD of July 29, 1937.
TUNA FISHING AND THE 75-PER
CENT LAW
A few days ago announcement was made
of a concession granted by the Panama Gov-
ernment to a Japanese syndicate for tuna
fishing in Panama waters. Inasmuch as the
Japanese have thoroughly explored the pos-
sibilities for this kind of fishing in this lo-
cality, the presumption is that tuna are to be
found in worth-while quantities, and that the
hauls will be
pondingly large.
While tun
nin, perhaps, does not
conflict material.
'business of Pa-
nama fishermen
ish mackerel, repper and corbina, it
would seem no more than right that the 75-
per cent iaw which is now applied to nearly
all concessions exploitation of which in-
volve the use of labor, s h o uld obtain
in the case of the tuna concessionaires, this
opinion being based on the understanding
that the Japanese expect to work the fish-
eries with men of their own race.
To be eminently fair to everybody, the
law should have a universal application.

a concern formed by Japanese residents of ranama about a year

Panama, the fish to be sent to a cannery at San Pedro, California.
General manager of the company 1s HIXOKUMA S IRAISFI, who has
been associated with Japanese fisheries at San Pedro for over
20 years.
2. Hikokumm Shiraishi just referred to was aboard the "Amano"
in the capacity of purser. The vessel was manned by a crew of 14
Japanese, who were recruited by Shiraishi in Japan. They were all said
to be expert fishermen, ranging in ages from 26 to 37 years. The master
of the vessel was CHRISTIAN S. ALBERTSEN, of Danish ancestry, who is a
citizen of Panamm and who claimed he had been working for Mr. Amano in
the fishing busimess in Panam for some time. Mr. Albertsen stated he
had been in Japan for six months awaiting completion of the "Amano."

111.55 gross tems; 9.5 kmets speed (maximmm); 25(h.P. Diesel engines;

4. Mr. Shiraishi appeared to be considerably interested
in the local fisheries. He mentioned to local Japanese fishermen the
probability of Japanese fishing vessele from the Pacific coast working
Hawaiian waters in the near future and indicated that he intended to
contimme researah work in this connection during the "Amano's" voyage
to the Pacific coast. The only drawback to tuna fishing operations in
Hawaiian wmaters, he stated, is the lack of sardines or other suitable
small fish for bait.
5. After taking on 4,42l gallons of Diesel oil and other
supplies, the "Ammno" left Honolulu at 6:00 A.u., July 2, 1937. It
was reported that she was to proceed to the California coast, fishing
en route, and to deliver her catch to the Van Camp Sea Food Company,
which concern is understood to have guaranteed the vessel's credit for
the supplies she took on here. The vessel is then to proceed to Paneme.
6. This office has fust received from local Coast Guard
officials a copy of an intercepted radio message from the Japanese
vessel "Mino(wa) Maru" at Masatlan, Mexico, to the Amano Fishery Com-
pany at Panama City, dated July (Jume) 15, 1937, which 1s suggestive
of projected large-scale Japanese operations in Mexico, with the Amano
Company of Panama as an essential element in the scheme. The message
is as follows:
"MINO(WA) MARU clearing from Mazatlan on lBth, bound home.
Relations with us fer past year have become very cordial. We
have been granted permission to contimue our work. The com-
plete support of Mexico has been further petitioned. and we
hope it will contimme to be given. we leave this country with
a good opportunity for future suecess and Japan's originel aim
is assured. Pray for contimmed support."
This message takes on additional interest in the light of the denial
made here by Mr. Christian S. Albertsen, master of the "Amano." that
the vessel had any connection with other Japanese fishing interests on
the Pacifio coast. It is also significant that the establishment of
the Amano Company is reported to have received authorative (authorita-
tive) sanetiom from Japan. The faet that the "Amano" is desigmed to
feed a mether ship may also be considered as indicative of proposed
expansiom of Japanese fishery interests along the Pacific coast of the
United States. Mexico and Central America.

Cerps Area and Panama.

Clipping from THE PANAMA AMERICAN of October 22, 1937.
Bar Sino-Jap
Ship Registry
In Panama
Measure Taken To
Avoid Trouble
With Belligerents
Provisional suspension of the
right of registering Chinese and
Japanese ships under the Pan-
amanian flag has been ordered
by the Government of Panama.
according to an announcemen
made Thursday by Jose E. Le-
fevre, Secretary of the Panam:
Department of Foreign Rela
tons.
The order assertedly has been
issued as a measure to prevent
possible diplomatic conflict:
with the warring powers of th?
Far East.
Issuance of the order follows
the receipt of a Japanese note
stating that the Japanese Gov-
ernment had approved Septem-
ber 18 the proclamation of the
Commander-in-Chief of the
Third Division of the Japanese
Imperial Fleet, whereby the
China Sea was closed to all
Chinese ships.
'As. a precautionary measure.
the note stated, the Japanese
Governmen; had decided to in-
clude in the Japanese Navy pro-
clamation of August 15, all Chi-
nese ships obtaining foreign re-
gistry after its issuance.
The note was forwarded to
the Panama Department of
Foreign Relations by the Jap-
anese Consulate here on Sep-
tember 28.
A similar note, it was stared.
was sent to all countries inter-
ested in navigation and mari-
time commerce on the China
Sea.
Following a lengthy study of
the matter, the Panamanian
Government decided to suspend
provisionally the registration of
Chinese and Japanese shins
until foreign powers affected by
the Japanese decision agree on
joint action on the question.

Enclosure No. 2, to Despatch No.53-.of Octobera3-1937.
Clipping from THE PANAMA AMERICAN of Uctober 16, 1937.
What Price Fish?
Onè does not need to be a military or & naval
rist, or an expert on foreign affairs, to know
that the intelligence services of all countries in the
world now are functioning as they never functioned
before....
Agents bf all nationalities are working themselves
into a lather ferreting out information. Some of it they
get through their own initiative and personal observa-
tion and some through spending large sums of money
in the proper quarters. All of it, for what it is worth,
is sent back tó their principals in the country to which
they belong and there the wheat is sorted from the
chaff.
The world is a powder keg and Mussolini, Hitler
and the Mikado are running around frantically with
matches, endeavoring to set off the blast which they
hope will accomplish their basic purpose of demolish-
ing the solid institutions of Democracy.
It does not require the imagination of a fiction
writer to conceive that the Isthmus of Panama, at the
present time, must be a seething cauldron of foreign
espionage. The Canal will be one of the first objectives
of naval attack in the world conflagration which is
approaching with terrifying rapidity
— and at any
time, today, tomorrow or next week, it may be target
of saboteurs whose code of ethics does not require that
they wait for a formal declaration of war.
Because of these facts, The Panama American has
pursued a policy of bringing to public attention any
foreign activity which might be construed as being
against the best interests of the United States and her
good neighbor and inseparable ally, the Republic of
Panama. Such a situation is the following:
Yoshitaro Amano, a local Japanese merchant who
strives to give the appearance of being in business "in
a small way," is now in Los Angeles to receive a new
"fishing" ship of the latest type, recently constructed
in Japan for work in Panamanian waters.
The ship is named the Amano Maru. It is 98 feet
overall, 111 gross tons, has a spéed' of around 10 knots
and is equipped with 350 H.P. Diesel engines and a
powerful radio receiving and sending apparstus. The
ship will arrive here in the near future to begin "fish-
ing" operations.
Now this modest, retiring unobtrusive Japanese
gentleman who is "in business in a small way" is in
reality a millionaire. He is president of the Amano
Marine Products Company and has valuable interests
throughbut Central and. South America. On reliable
authority it is stated that one of his properties in
Chile is valued at $750,000-a mere three quarters of a
million dollars!!
Press reports, published a couple of months ago,
recorded the formation of a company to engage in
the fishing ndastry in Panama. President of the Com-
that Mr. Amano is the whole works and that the other
stockholders, while they. do exist in fact, are merely
dummies in whatever game it is that this modest
Japanese businessman is playing.
It is a safe prediction that. when the Amano Maru
arrives here, investigation will show that she is man-
ned by a crew of officers in the naval reserve - as
is the case of most Jana
authorities who by
and not m

Mr. Harry Stuhr Is  Brbstol Bay fisherman
and an organizer in the Alaskan fishermen's
unions. He has recently become prominent in
fishing circles through his activities in conm
nection with the threat of Japanese fishing
4
operations in Alaskan waters. More particu-
larly, Mr. Stuhr appears to be closely con-
nected with a recently reported movement on
the Pacific Coast to boycott the handling of
2
certain Jepanese ocean freight cargo, if no
other means is found to deal with possible
Japanese encroachment in Alaskan fishing
waters -- particularly Bristol Bay.
Mr. Stuhr called on Mr. Sturgeon on
October 4 and stated that he had been selected
by his union to come to Washington to see what
he could find out in regard to steps being
taken by the Department to deal with the sal-
mon situation in Bristol Bay. Mr. Stuhr was
informed that the Secretary and other executive-
officers of the Department had been giving
close study to this situation and were earnestly
endeavoring to work out a plan of ad justme
through which American fishing interests and
American fishermen would be protected. Mr
Stuhr emphasized that he wished to have as uc
definite information as possible on what was
being done by the Department. He was infored
by Mr. Sturgeon that everything possible, under
the

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
the circumstances, was being done; that
special effort was being made to expedite
n adjustment; but that it was not the prac-
tice of the Department to discuss in detail
a matter of this kind prior to or during the
course of its consideration with a foreign
Government. Mr. Stuhr was also reminded
that the fishermen's unions had given Mr.
Sturgeon, during his recent visit to the
Coast, the impression that no agitation cal-
culated to hamper or possibly defeat the
efforts of the Department would be engaged
in for the time being; it was further sug-
gested that strike or boycott action aimed
to interrupt the flow of Japanese ocean
cargo at Pacific ports would undoubtedly
not be in the best interests of the fisher-
men themselves.
Mr. Dimond, Delegate to Congress from
Alaska, and two representatives of the canned
salmon packing industry of the Pacific Coast
were present and took part in the conversa-
tion with Mr. Stuhr, which is being more
fully recorded in another memorandum.

F/W
I have to report that the Panamanian Consul reports
that the Panamanian Government is opposed to the extension
a
of Japanese fishing interests off the coast of Panama and
has succeeded in stopping the extension of those interests
in the following manner.
Upon the outbreak of the military conflict in China
in

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
in July and as a result of the action of the Japanese
naval euthorities egainst Chinese shipping a number of
Chinese vessels were given Panamanian registry. The
Japanese Foreign Office epproached the Panamanian Consul
and stated that the Japanese Government did not approve
of such an evasion of their "blockade" by the Chinese
and might heve to interfere with such ships in Chinese
waters. The Panamanian Consul replied that under Pana-
manian laws such transfers were legal, that & Japanese
fishing vessel had just been given Panemanian registry
and thet since no declaration of war had been made
between China end Japan his Government would protest
any interference on the part of the Japanese with ves-
sels under Panamanian registry. He told the Japanese
authorities that the Panamanian Government had success-
fully maintained its rights against the interference of
the United States Coast Guard with its vessels along
the American coast during the prohibition era and had
obtained indemnities for losses suffered as a result of
such interference. It therefore expected to maintain
its rights against any other powerful nation such as
Japan. He added that if Panamanian vessels should be
interfered with measures might be teken against Japanese
trade with Panama in order to enforce Panamanian olaims
for damages.
The Panamanian Consul states that his Government
then agreed, at Panama, with the Japanese that no more
Chinese vessels would be given Panamanian registry on
the

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
the condition that Panamanian registry would also be
denied to all Japanese vessels. It therefore appears
that no more Japanese fishing vessels will be permitted
to operate in Panamanian waters. The Consul does not
know whether action will be taken in cancelling the
registration of the AMANO MARU.
I enclose as of possible interest a copy of an
article in the PANAMA AMERICAN, of Panama, Saturday,
October 16, 1937, regarding the AMANO MARU. The article
was furnished by the Panamanian Consul at Yokohama.
Very respectfully yours,
Richard F. Boyce,

C.ILIVSPAPER
AN INDELJDENT1
Panama American
PANAMA, R. P., SATURDAY, OCTOBER 16, 1937
What Price Fish?
One does not need to be a military or a naval
strategist, or an expert on foreign affairs, to know
that the intelligence services of all countries in the
world now are functioning as they never functioned
before.
Agents of all nationalities are working themselves
into a lather ferreting out information. Some of it they
get through their own initiagiv and personal observa-
tion and some through sp mding large sums of -money
in the proper quarters All' of it, for what it is worth.
is sent back to their principals in the country to which
they belong and there the wheat is sorted from the
chaff.
The world Is a powder keg and Mussolini, Hitler
and the Mikado are running around frantically with
matches, endeavoring to set off the blast which they
hope will accomplish their basic purpose of demolish-
ing the solid institutions of Democracy.
It does not require the imagination of a fiction
writer to conceive that the Isthmus of Panama, at the
present time, must be a seething cauldron of foreign
espionage. The Canal will be one of the first objectives
of naval attack in the world conflagration which is
approaching with terrifying rapidity — and at any
time, today, tomorrow or next week, it may be target
of saboteurs whose code of ethics does not require that
they wait for a formal declaration of war.
Because of these facts, The Panama American has
pursued a policy of bringing to public attention any
foreign activity which might be construed as being
against the best interests of the United States and her
good neighbor and inseparable ally, the Republic of
Panama. Such a situation is the following:
Yoshitaro Amano, a local Japanese merchant who
strives to give the appearance of being in business "in
a small way," is now in Los Angeles to receive a new
"fishing" ship of the latest type, recently constructed
in Japan for work in Panamanian waters.
The ship is named the Amano Maru. It is 98 feet
overall, 111 gross tons, has a speed of around 10 knots
and is equipped with 350 H.P. Diesel engines and a
powerful radio receiving and sending apparatus. The
ship will arrive here in the near future to begin "fish-
ing" operations.
Now this modest, retiring unobtrusive Japanese
gentleman who is "in husiness in a small way" is in
reality a millionaire. He is president of the Amano
Marine Products Company and has valuable interests
throughout Central and South America. On reliable
authority it is stated that one of his properties in
Chile is valued at $750,000—a mere three quarters of a
million dollars!!
Press reports, published a couple of months ago,
recorded the formation of a company to engage in
the fishing industry in Panama. President of the Com-
pany is Yoshitaro Amano and associated with him are
Tiyosharu Kitamaru, Masao Kokuma and Yoshito Sada-
jiro. The Panama American's information however, is
that Mr. Amano is the whole works and that the other
stockholders, while they do exist in fact, are merely
dummies in whatever game it is that this modest
Japanese businessman is playing.
It is a safe prediction that when the Amano Maru
arrives here, investigation will show that she is man-

341 of November 24, 1937, regarding the motorship
Al ANC MARU, for which a Japanese company headed by
Mr. Yoshitaro Amano obtained a Panamanian registry
to enable the ship to engage in deep-sea fishing in

Mr. Julio Briceno, Consul Jeneral of Panama in
Yokohama, has informed me tiat tne Panamanian Govern-
ment has decided to forbid Japanese interests from
FILED
engaging in deep-sea fishing in Fanamanian waters.
33
A newspaper item in the JAPAN ADVERTISER of January
F
9, 1938, states that the Panama Government will suspend
A1
fishing privileges to all foreigners. Mr. Briceno
says that the Japanese are the only foreigners engaged
in such fishing.
Mr. Briceno says that he recommended such action
to his Government. He based his argument on his belief
that the Japanese would soon have a monopoly control
over

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.99)
2
over fishing in Panamanian waters; that once the
Japanese secured a footing in Panamanian business
or industry they would try to control more and more
and could not be disloged. He told his Government
that the Japanese, far from appreciating the opportunities
which Latin American countries might make available
to Japanese immigrants, soon take the attitude that
such opnortunities are vested Japanese rights rather
than privileges. As proof of this he sent his Govern-
ment a clipoing from an  nglish-language newspaper of Tokyo
which quoted a Japanese newspaper editorial protesting
against Brazilian restrictions against Japanese immi-
grants and saying that as Brazil is a land of negroes
it needs Japanese immigrants to build up the country.
Mr. Briceno says that the President of the
Amano company tried unsuccessfully to obtain Costa
Rican registry for his ship and permission to operate
in Costa Rican waters.

LEGATION OF THE

San José, Costa Rica,

No.273
Subject:
Japanese Activities in Costa Rica.
36
2
2
9338
394.628/199
Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to my despatch No.264 of
May 21, 1938, and to report as follows:
At the Argentine Legation last night I asked
Colonel Cañas, Chier Aide to the President, as to the
truth of the newspaper reports to the effect that Mr.
Amano is here for the purpose of reviving the matter of
obtaining Costa Rican registry for a Japanese ship of the
same name. He replied that the matter had been taken up
with him personally by Mr. Amano only a few days previous
and that the latter was most insistent that the Costa Rioan
Government

=== Page 102 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Government reverse itselr on the matter of registry.
Colonel Cañas then added that like the President, he
viewed with suspicion the activities of the Japanese in
Costa Rica, was personally convinced that proximity to the
Canal Zone is their real objective, rather than cotton
growing and fishing, and referred to their recent lease of
property eight miles east of Puntarenas which he described
as being only two miles from the Gulf of Nicoya. H e
stated that the President had thus far resisted the
Japanese proposals for registry of the steamer AMANO and
would continue to do so unless the situation became so
acute that no other course appeared to be open to him. He
added that while some Costa Ricans have faith in the cotton
growing proposals, he personally believed that the failure
in Alajuela would be duplicated on the Pacific Coast.
An effort to register the Amano Fishery Company, S.A.,
has been made by Mr. Amano, but due to some defect in the
articles the application for registry has thus far been
1/
denied. I enclose a memorandum on this subject from Clerk
Fernando Peña, who was detailed to eheck the records.
Respectfully yours,
Wm d tarniliooh
WH. Hornibrook.
Enclosure: Memorandum

Enclosure to Despatch No. 273 of May 27, 1938.
MEMORANDUM FOR THE MINISTER:
AMANO FISHERY COMPANY, S. A.
(Civil Registry Pending File, Vol. 163, Entry 578, Journal: 163/578.)
The registration has not as yet been completed, as the Civil
Registry finds the application defective in that it does not give
any reference to the effect that the te el transferred by Yoshitaro
Amano to the Amano Fishery Co., S. A.been registered under the
Costa Rican Registry, in accordance with the provisions of Art. 105
of the Law of Mercantile Societies.
The constitutive document states that the name of the firm will
be as above given; that in Spanish its legal name is "SOCIEDAD PES-
QUERA AMANO, S. A."; that it will engage in the fishery business in
all its forms; that the Company reserves itself the right to buy
goods and chattels and real estate; to exploit any property in con-
nection with the operation of its business; to acquire any concess-
ions and rights for the good operation and management of the firm.
Term of operation: 4 years renewable for six periods of
4 years each (24 years)
Legal domicile: San Jose, Costa Rica.
Social Capital: $1o,O0o U. S. Cy. divided in 10 shares of
$1,000 each.
The Company will operate the vessel "NOTONAVE AMANO-A-lE";
steel hull - net tonnage: 49.83; 250 HP Diesel Engine;
Constructed at Miho Shimiza, Japan, in 1937.
Executive Board:
President, Fernando Soto Guardia.
Secretary, Fernando Soto Harrison.
Chairmen: Rita Harrison Tinoco, Nicolas Rojas Melendez
and Jose Rojas Melendez.
Legally, the firm cannot start operations until its regis-
tration is passed by the Civil Registry.
FRP.

Dear Mr. Cummings:
Re: Japan's Whaling Industry.
1t1
We believe that you will be interest'eawin the
following information which came from Tokyo recarling
plans for whaling in the Arctic by the Japanese, which
we pass on for your infornation in case it has escaped
your attention:
"According to information through the press
the Arctic Whaling Co., which 1s formed Jointly
894.6
894.628
by the companies Nippon Fishing Co. and
Hayashikan Shoten, has decided in August or
September to send the floating factory ship
"Tonan Maru" (ex "Antarctic"), previously bought
from Norway, which at present is carrying on
2
whaling in the Antarctic, to the Arctic to take
28/200
up whaling there. Such catching North of 70
degrees has up to now precticelly been monopol-
ized by Russian whalers, and keen competition
must therefore be expected from their side.
It is informed that tne above mentioned
resolution hes been made on the ground of
experiences gainea by the vessel "Yuk1 Maru",
which the company at the end of July last year
sent out to the Eering Sea to investigate the
conditions, and which remained up there for a
couple of months. During the expedition this
vessel saw hundreds of whales, and on the
whole the stock of whales proved to be so
constant that whaling there after modern
methods ought to be profitable. It will be
interesting to see how the experiment thus
planned will fall out."
Very truly yours,

Dear Mr. Carr:
In regard to Consular Report No. 3060 entitled Pearls, A
894.628 PEARLS/2
Study of Their Culture and Marketing" written by Consul C. O.
(C2)
Spamer, Tokyo, Japan, dated October 14, 1933:
Ca
894.67
The report is of such an outstanding quality there can be
no doubt but that a decided contribution has been made to the
literature of pearl culture. Every phase of the subject from
production to ultimate marketing has been treated clearly, com-
prehensively and in a manner which leaves no further inquiry
necessary on the part of the industry, and should be of great
interest to the layman. It is believed that such a report might
well be used as a model.
The report has been submitted to one of the outstanding
jewelers' magazines with permission to publish, if desired.
It would be appreciated if a copy of this letter be placed
in the personal file of Consul Spamer.
Very truly yours,
Frederic D. Grab,
Liaison Officer.

From:
Commander, Bering Sea Patrol Force.
To:
Commandant.
Subject: Japanese Government fishing vessel "HAXUHo MARU".
1.
The HAkUHO MARU called at Attu on June 26, made engine repairs and
watered ship and left early June 29, after probably locati g the UNALGA bound
for that island, and which vessel arrived at Attu at 5 p.m. of that day, June 29.
2.
On June 30 the HAIUH0 MARU sent a nosition report 49º25' N 175°38' E.
3.
It being renorted by the Commissioner of Fisheries that the HAkUHO
MARU was at Atka, thr HAIDA was despatched there and arrived on July 14.
4.
The Commanding Officer of the HAIDA reports that the HAKUHo "ARU
arrived at Atka from Amchitka on July 4, and left July 8 for Attu.
5.
It was learned from Mr. Schroeder that she arrived in Attu and
wanted to fish, but this being a fox farm island the Commander was advised that
fishing was only oermitted by the natives.
It was later found that the
Japanese had robbed the native nets.
6.
While at Atke it was claimed that crew of the "AkUC MARU was com-
V
pesed of Japanese technical s tudents, and that one mining engineer was
on board.
7.
At Attu it was claimed that the vessel was examining fish for food
and for seals.
8.
This same vessel was renorted in these same localities last
season, and was fishing off St.Lawrence Island in 1926, and I think called at
Unalaska during the latter season, tho the records are not at hand.
9.
It would seem proner thet the Japanese Government should obtain
permission from this Government to make these irvestigations, and that
the Commander o this Force be advised if such permission has been granted.
(Sgd.) C.S.COCHRAN

FEB 13 1930
Dept ofohn D. Hickerson,
Division of Western European Affairs,
Room 3022,State Department,
Washington, D.C.
S i r:
I am inclosing herewith a copy of a letter
addressed by the Commander of the Behring Sea Patrol
Force to the Commandant on 29 August, 1929, concerning
the activities of the Japanese schooner HAkUHO MARU.
The remainder of the letter is self-explanatory.
In the early days of the seal patrol Great
Britain, Japan and Russia, as well as the United States,
maintained gunboats and revenue cutters in Behring Sea to
prevent pelagic sealing but the vessels of the other powers
were gradually withdrawn and during latter years the entire
responsibility for the seal patrol has devolved upon the
United States, and the high contracting parties in the
treaty proclaimed December 14, 191l (Treaty Series 564)
have entirely withdrawn their armed vessels even in the
patrol of seal islands in which they are particularly
interested.
Under the circumstances it would seem that the
Japanese government should advise this Department when
ever it intends to send one of its vessels into the
Behring Sea.
Very truly yours,
Port
Root
Inclosure

at the beginning of June, next, and will touch at Dutch Har-
bor and the Pribilof Islands, and you request to be advised
if this Department would be disposed to consider that these
students can be classed as coming within the provisions of
Section I (2) of the Executive Order of February 21, 1928.
This Section provides that aliens who are through passengers
on vessels touching at ports of the United States may land
3
temporarily under regulations prescribed by the Department
of Labor without documents of any kind. The regulations
authorized under this Section of the Executive Order may be
found in Paragraph 6, Subdivision H, of Rule 3 of the Immi-
gration Rules of January 1, 1930.
In reply thereto, I have to advise you that so
MAY 21193
far as this particular vessel is concerned, this Department
FICHO
has no objection to placing these aliens under the classi-
fication mentioned in your communication. A copy of this
letter is being sent to the District Director of Immigration
at Ketchikan, Alaska, the Pribilof Islands and Dutch Harbor
being located in his District.
I am, Sir, with great respeet,
ROBE CARL WHITE
Assistant Secretary.

Reference is made to the attaohed note, dated

A
April 30, 1930, from the Japanese Embassy concerning
the proposed fishing cruise off the coast of Alaska
of the "Hakuyo Maru" training ship of the College
of Fisheries of the Japanese Government, having on
board 32 students in addition to the regular crew.
Paragraph 315 of General Instruction Consular
No. 936 provides that except in the case of warships,
masters of vessels of all nationalities sailing for
any fort under the jurisdiction of the United States
(except a port in the Panama Canal Zone or the Philip-
pine Islands) must submit a list of all of the alien
6
the
members of the vessel's crew to/American consular
officer at the port from which the vessel commences its
voyage.
Under Paragraph 3 of Rule 7 (A) of the Rules of
MAY 211930
the Department of Labor of January 1, 1930, the
F
expression "arriving in the United States from any
foreign port or place" means "arriving in the United:
States, and any waters, territory or other, place
subject

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.95)
-2
subject to the jurisdiction thereof", etc.
It would therefore seem that if, as appears
likely, the tra ining ship in question is not a
"warship" it should have a visaed crew list.
Since there seeme to be question regarding the
no
possibility of obtaining a crew list, it does not
appear that the requirement may properly be waived.
With regard to the students, there is attached
a suggested letter to the Department of Labor stating
that the Department would appreciate being advised
whether the Department of Labor would be disposed to
consider that the students, for the purposes mentioned
in the Embassy's note, come within the provisions of
Section II (2) of Executive Order of February 21, 1928
relative to the temporary admission without documents
of aliens who are through passengers on vessels touching
at ports of the United States.

The receipt is acknowledged of the note of the Departmeft of
State dated May 27, 1930, file FE 894.628 - Vessels/43, in regard
2
to a proposed cruise to be made this year by the Japanese vessel
Hakuho Maru, belonging to the Department of Agriculture and Fores-
try, off the western coast of the Aloutian Islands for the purpose
of taking observations of the customary routes followed by seals
and of methods employed in enforcing police regulations against
poaching.
Pursuant to your request the Bureau of Fisheries has in-
structed its appropriate representatives to the end that the usual
courtesies and facilities may be extended to the vessel and its
personnel.
It is suggested that the attention of the Secretary of the
力
Treasury be called to the proposed cruise of the Hakuho Maru with
8
the view that the Coast Guard keep in touch with the vessel. It
is understood that the Hakuho Maru has made a number of trips in
recent seasons to the general region of the Aleutian Islands and
Bering Sea. On some of these trips the vessel has brought a Jap-
anese observer to the Pribilof Islands, but the reasons for re-
peated extended cruises in western Alaska are not understood. It
is believed that as a result of further observations by Coast
Guard vessels more might very properly be learned in regard to the
real objectives of these frequent and extended trips of the vessel
1N 24193.
in question.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
heolrn't of suito
Again referring to previous correspondence in respect to the activ-
ities of Japanese fishing vessels in waters of Bering Sea off the coast
of Alaska, the Department of State is hereby advised that a letter, under
date of July 5, 1930, from Superintendent Harry J. Christoffers of the
-VE
Bureau of Fisheries, St. Paul Island, Pribilof Islands, Alaska, has been
received as follows:
S
The Japanese training ship, Hakuyo Maru of the Imperial
S
SELS
Fisheries Institute, Tokyo, called at the island on July 4
and requested permission for the officers and crew and stu-
dents to visit the rookeries. They were all shown Gorbatch
rookery on July 5. The vessel left for Kamchatka during the
afternoon.
5
9
This vessel is used as a training ship for fourth year
students taking the fisheries course of the I perial Fisheries
Institute. The students are required to take three years
training at the university and the fourth year's training is
taken on this vessel. The ship spends the summers in northern
waters and the winters in southern waters.
The name should not be confused with that of the Hakuho
Maru, the fisheries patrol boat which called at this island
several times and which has been along the Aleutian Islands
only for the past several years. The officers of the Hakuyo
Maru know very little about the other boat and stated they did
not know where the vessel is cruising this year. The Captain
advised me that he did not know where the Hakuho Maru would be
this season but one of our other employees heard one of the
officers state that the Hakuho Maru intends to go only as far
as Attu this season. The latter's wireless has not been heard
at this station to date. It is understood that the Hakuyo Maru
1.11
intends to make a visit to this station every year with students.
32 students were aboard the vessel.
Any further important developments in connection with this matter will
be communicated without delay to your office.

628

1

VE
Fisheries, as follows:
S
Captain Andersen of the Alaska Commercial Company's
S
schooner Eunice of Unalaska advised that the Hakuho (not Hakuyo)
1.1
Maru visited Attu Island in June. Boats were sent ashore but
5
the white residents advised the Japs that the natives were sick
with measles and as soon as they understood this they inmediately
1
put back to the vessel. No more boats came ashore and the
5
Hakuho Maru soon left. Some think that the boat may have been
7
after live sea otter but they have no reason to assume this.
On returning from Attu, the Eunice went around the South
side of Amatignak Island (near Kiska, former naval reserve) and
there saw a Jap war vessel lying close in and apparently taking
bearings on the island. lie headed his boat toward the war vessel
but when they sighted him they turned around and put to sea. The
vessel's departure at that time may, however, have only been a
coincidence.
Mr. Shroeder, trader, and natives of Attu stated to Captain
Andersen that during the previous two seasons the Hakuho Maru
C:
stopped at Attu on her way from and to Japan. They said the ves-
C..
sel had a number of surf boats all well painted in the spring but
11
S
with bottoms badly torn, worn and scratched on their way back
showing they had been doing considerable beach work.
The Hakuyo Maru spent some time in 3ristol Bay before going
to Unalaska. They were fishing for crabs which they canned. A
can was opened on board ship and proved to be a much better
product than the Japanese crab.

May 22, 1933.

844.3311/167
With referenoe to the request of April
5, 1933, of the Japanese Ambassador that
the usual courtesies and facilities be af-
forded to the S.S. HAKUYO MARU and its
personnel when calling at the "ports of
Dutoh Harbor and the Pribilof Islands between
the middle of July and the middle of August",
the Navy Department informed this Department
in its letter of April 24, 1933, that its
view was that "the Japanese Government should
be requested not to propose visits to such
ports."
In the disoussions of the more recent
oase of the S. S. HAKUHO MARU, the Navy
Department has stated its view orally as
being that there would be no objeotion to
granting permission to Japanese Government
vessels which desire to visit ports in the
Aleutian and Pribilof Islands where American
officials are present. There are American
officials present at Dutch Harbor (Unalaska)
in the Aleutian Islands and at St. Paul in
the Pribilof Islands. From the attached
letter of May 3 from the Japanese Consul at
Seattle it is made olear that the port in the
Pribilof Islands which the HAKUYO MARU

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
desired to visit was St. Paul. There are
Amoriean offioialo at St Paul. It would
seem, therefore, that visits of the
HAKUYO MARU to St. Paul and Dutch Harbor
(Unalaska) would not be objeotionable to the
Navy Department in view of the Navy Depart-
ment's present attitude with regard to
visits of Japanese Government vessels.
Although permission to visit these ports
has already been withheld from the HAkUYO
MARU, it is suggested that, in view of the
fact that the HAKUYO MARU did not plan to
reach Unalaska (Dutch Harbor) until July 13,
this case might be reconsidered and the
Japanese Government be informed that, upon
further consideration, this Government has
found that it would now be convenient for
the HAKUYO MARU to visit the ports of
Unalaska (Dutoh Harbor) and St. Paul in the
Pribilof Islands where there are Amerioan
officials ready to receive the visiting
vessel. (Note: In the Aleutian Islands
there would appear to be an American official
only at the port of Unalaska (Dutoh Harbor).
This official is a representative of the
Department of Justice. There is also, part
of the time, an officer of the Publio Health
Servioe of the Treasury Department. In the
Pribilof Islands there is an Agent and Gare-
taker of the Bureau of Fisherles of the

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
Department of Commeroe at St. Paul and
another at St. George. From June to
Ogtober the Superintendent of the Pribilof
Islands, who during the rest of the year
is at Seattle, stays in the Pribilof Islands,
most of the time at St. Paul. It would seem,
therefore, that St. Paul and St. George
in the Pribilof Islands and Dutoh Harbor
(Unalaska) in the Aleutian Islands have
present Amerioan officials within the meaning
of the Navy Department.)

May 6, 1933.
Mr. Hamilton handed the original of
the attached informal memorandum to Mr.
Midzusawa of the Japanese Embassy. Mr.
Midzusawa read the statement carefully and
then read aloud the last three or four
lines of the statement and laughed and
commented that those lines sounded very
much like the reply the Japanese had made
to previous requests that American naval
vessels be permitted to visit the Japanese
mandated islands and that it seemed to be
a "50-50 proposition". Mr. Hamilton
joined in his laughter but made no comment.

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
1933
May 1, 1933.
894.3311/174
With reference to the letter of April 24
of the Navy Department to the effect that,
as the Japanese have stated that visits
of our vessels to the Japanese Mandate
Islands would be welcome only at those
islands where Japanese are present, for the
reasons that visits at the other islands
would be disturbing to the natives and
that it would furthermore be impolite on the
part of the Japanese not to have an official
present to extend the usual courtesies,
"it would be manifestly improper for this
Government to sanction the visit of a
Japanese public vessel at ports in the
Aleutian Islands where no United States of-
ficials will be present", -
This question was thoroughly gone into
in l929 from the point of view of American
vessels visiting the Japanese Mandate
Islands and a reply such as the Navy Depart-
ment proposes would be apparently a distinct
departure from policy. The United States
Government permits Japanese vessels to
visit all ports under American jurisdiction
excepting a few ports closed for military
reasons (memorandum of September 27, 1929,
of conversation between officers of the
Navy

=== Page 203 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
Navy Department and of FE). On December 20,
1930, the Department wrote to the Navy De-
partment that this Department is still of
the view that the matter of visits of
American naval vessels to the Japanese Man-
date Islands can best be worked out by
friendly approaches, at intervals, on the
part of the Embassy to the Japanese Foreign
Office. Mr. Neville reported in despatch
No. 1366 of December 16, 1929 that "If it
is desired to press the matter, there are,
it seems to me, two metrods of procedure,
each of them somewhat troublesome: one,
insist as a matter of treaty right, which
will raise the hole question of mandates
and our relation to them (but we could
probably gain our point if worth while to
do so); second, refuse permission to
Japanese vesseis to enter ports under our
jurisdiction except such as they have a
clear treaty right to enter. This would
probably have a decided influence on the
Japanese Navy; it would be a drastic step
and a departure from our historic policy,
but it very likely would be effective in the
long run".
The refusal of permission for this ves-
sel of the Tokyo Fishing Institute to visit
American ports, other than those specifically
closed, might not be legally sound. In the
memorandum

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
memorandum of September 27, 1929, mentioned
above, it was pointed out that ail ports
in the United States were open, except those
specifically closed, while in Japan the
only open ports were ports of entry for
trade purpose. In a memorandum of the
office of the Legal Adviser the conclusion
was advanced that the United States had
no legal ground to complain of the exclusion
of its vessels from those places which Japan
had declared to be not open to foreign com-
merce. At the above-mentioned meeting of
officers of the Navy Department and FE it
was agreed that it would be preferable at
that time at least, not to enter into the
question of legal rights. It may also be
that further question would arise from the
fact that, whereas the Japanese Gov ernment
presumably treats the vessels of all foreign
nations similarly in connection with visits
to the Mandate Islands, our refusal of per-
mission of the visit of the Japanese vessel
in question would be a limitation directed
toward one nation only.
Although I can understand the satisfaction
which we would gain from refusing permission
for the visit of this vessel in question to
Dutoh Harbor and the Pribiloff Islands, it
would be, in my opinion, in consideration of
the

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
the foregoing facts, preferable not to inject
into the question of the visit of American
vessels to the Japanese Mandate Islands the
question of the visit of Japanese vessels
to American islands in the vicinity of
Alaska, at least not until such time as we
are ready to press the Japanese Government
with regard to the question of visits of
American vessels to the Japanese Mandate
Islands. Considering the fact that it was
not considered desirable to press the
question in 1929 and 1930, it would seem
to be even less desirable now to open up
the question.
(The most recent case of Japanese request-
ing permission to visit Dutch Harbor and of
permission being granted occurred in August,
1928.)
It is suggested, therefore, that the
question be taken up informally with the ap-
propriate officers of the Navy Depertment.
Am.m..
LES:KC

Subjeot: Visit of HAKUYO MARU to Amerioan ports.
894
894, 624
Commander Lammers stated that the Navy Department
felt that in view of the Japanese attitude with regard to
9
2
the visit of American vessels to ports in the Japanese
Mandate Islands where Japanese officials are not present,
C0
m
S
permission should not be granted to the HAKUYO MARU to
中
SSELS/73
visit Dutch Harbor and Pribiloff Islands, but that permis-
sion should be granted only for visits to ports of the
United States proper and the territory of Hawaii. He
also said that the visit of the HAKUYO MARU was further
regarded by the Navy Department as undesirable inasmuch
as the HAKUYO MARU would undoubtedly make hydrographio
surveys and other studies which would be of strategic
JUL 5-1933
value to the Japanese in case of war. Commander Lammers
FILED
added that, although the Japanese undoubtedly have a
great deal of information of this nature, it would be
preferable not to give them this additional opportunity
for investigation. Commander Lammers suggested that the
Japanese

=== Page 215 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
Japanese authorities be informed that the visit of the
HAKUYO MARU to the Aleutian and Pribiloff Islands would
not be granted for the reason that it would be improper
for this Government to grant such permission in view of
the faot that it would be impolite because of the absence
of any Amerioan officials in those ports to welcome the
HAKUYO MARU. Mr. Hornbeok replied that if the Navy
Departmont bolieved that the visit of the HAKUYO MARU
would give the Japanese information of strategic value
which they ought not to have, the Japanese authorities
should be informed that it was desired that the visit to
the Aleutian and Pribiloff Islands be omitted.
Mr. Hornbeok further said that, in his opinion, it would
be preferable not to give the reason advanced by the
Navy Department as the exouse of impoliteness had not
been advanoed in writing by the Japanese in connection
with visits to the Mandate Islands. Commander Lammers
was informed that refusal of this visit might very
probably be given unfortunate publicity in Japan but
that, of course, if it was a question of protecting
information of strategic value, nothing more could be
said. Mr. Hornbeok pointed out that in his opinion
refusal of this visit would make it less probable that
the Japanese would at some future time permit the visit
of

=== Page 216 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
of Amerioan vessels to ports in the Mandate Islands where
officials were not present for the reason that the Japa-
nose consider the strategic value of those Islands of
more importanoe to them than knowledge of the Aleutian
and Pribiloff Islands. Mr. Hornbeck was of the opinion
that the disadvantages of refusal of permission to the
HAKUYO MARU would outweigh any advantages which might
acorue. Commander Lammers said that he would take up
the matter again with Admiral Pratt and inform
Mr. Hornbeck of the Admiral's decision.
Subsequently Mr. Hornbeck was informed that the
Navy Department desired that permission not be granted
to the HAKUYO MARU to visit the Aleutian and Pribiloff
Iolands.

With reference to the request of
the Japanese Embassy that the usual
courtesies and facilities be afforded
to the Japanese soouting ship HAKUHO
MARU of the Department of Agriculture
and Forestry in case it should call
at ports in the Aleutian Islands for
shelter and provisions, --
Commander Lammers advised in a
conversation with Mr. Salisbury that,
unless there are important reasons
contra, such as possible effect on
the Fur Seal Convention, tht in the
opinion of the Navy Department a
reply should be made similar to that
made on May 6 to the Japanese Embassy
with regard to the proposed visit of
the HAKUYO MARU, with the additional
statement that there would be no ob-
jeotion to visits to those ports at
which there are American officials,
with the exception of the closed port
of Kiska. He based this conclusion
on the facts that American vessels
cannot visit ports in the Japanese
Mandate Islands where there are no
Japanese officials, that the natives
of the Aleutian Islands are dis-
turbed by such visits and that the
Islands are of strategic interest.
After

=== Page 218 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
After communication with the
various Government Departments which
might have representatives in the
Aloutian Islands, it appears that
Unalaska (sometimes referred to as
Dutch Harbor) is the only port where
there are American officials which
might be taken in the sense that the
Navy means, unless possibly Kodiak,
where there is an army radio station.
Kodiak, however, is separated from the
mainland of Alaska only by a narrow
strip of water and permission to visit
this place under the circumstances
would scarcely be of interest to the
Japanese. As the Aleutian Islands ex-
tend for 1200 miles,permission to visit
only Unalaska would not, it would seem,
be very satisfactory to the Japanese.

that the note was on its way and that if there
was anything in the note which was not clear
to Mr. Midzusawa Mr. Salisbury would be very
glad to call at the Embassy to talk the matter
over.
Shortly afterwards, Mr. Midzusawa telephoned

point which was not olear, namely, that although
the HAKUYO MARU had been granted permission to
visit not only tne port of Unalaska in the
Aleutian Islands but also the ports of st. Paul
and St. George in the Pribilof islands the
HAKUHO MARU had been grantea permission to visit
only the port of Unalaska. mr. salisbury re-
plied that whereas the Japanese Eabassy had re-
quested permission for the HAKUYO MARU to visit
both the Aleutian and the Pribilof Islands the
request with regard to the HAKUHO MARU had been
confined to the Aleutian Islands. Mr. Salisbury
stated that he was confident that should the
Japanese authorities desire pernission for the
HAKUHO MARU to visit the ports of St. Paul and
St. George in the Pribilof islands it would be
granted.

=== Page 239 === (confidence 1.00)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
May 12, 1933.
With regard to the note of May 9, 1933,
from the Japanese Ambassador, requesting
that courtesies and facilities be extended
to the HAKUHO MARU in case it should call
at ports of the Aleutian Islands for
shelter and provisions, -
It should be recalled that the preserva-
tion of the herds of seal is regarded by
this Government as important and that,in
case permission is not granted to this
vessel, the Japanese might seize this as
a pretext for denouncing the Fur Seal
Treaty of l9ll. The Japanese have for some
time desired a modification of the terms
of this Treaty, the argument of the Japanese
being that the seals have so increased in
number that they are consuming edible fish.
It is thought that the Japanese desire to
reduce the herd or stabilize it. Denuncia-
tion of the Treaty would adversely affect
American interests.
A further onsideration in this matter
is that in atiampting to remove the menace
to the American salmon industry occasioned
by Japanese fishing interests in Alaskan
waters, the United States might at some
time desire to make use of the Fur Seal
Treaty

=== Page 240 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Treaty in connection with protection of
the Americen salmon industry: th altera-
tion of the Fur Seal Treaty might be con-
ceded by this Government in order to
obtain Japanese agreement with regard to
Japanese fishing activities in Alaskan
waters. Were the Treaty denounced by the
Japanese, this Government would lose a
possible point for negotiation.
The only Article of the Fur Seal Treaty
of l9ll which might possibly be construed as
having a bearing on the present case is
Article VIII which reads:
"All of the High Contract-
ing Parties agree to cooperate
with each other in taking such
measures as may be appropriate and
available for the purpose of
preventing pelagic sealing in the
prohibited area mentioned in Article I."
It is recalled that the cruises of the
HAKUHO MARU along the Aleutians in 1929 and
1930 were reported by the Commander of the
Bering Sea Patrol Force of the Coast Guard
to have very much disturbed both natives
and whites along the Aleutians.
S:KC

Mr. Salisbury called on Mr. Bower at the Department
628-VESSELS/76
of Commerce and showed him a copy of the note of May 9,
1933, from the Japanese Embassy requesting that usual
courtesies and facilities be afforded to the Japanese
scouting ship HAKUHO MARU of the Department of Agriculture
and Forestry in case it should call at ports in the
Aleutian Islands for shelter and provisions. Mr. Bower
stated that, in his view, refusal to grant permission to
the HAKUHO MARU might have unfortunate results, might create
a tendenoy on the part of the Japanese to be less cooperative
with regard to the protection of fur seals and with regard
to the questions relating to the fishing industry generally.
In case the Japanese should use the refusal as a pretext for
denouncing the Fur Seal Treaty of l9ll - a modification
FILED
of this Treaty has been desired by the Japanese for some
5
16
time -
3

=== Page 244 === (confidence 0.98)
一 2 -
time - it would result in the free killing of fur seals and
a return to the conditions that existed before the signing
of the Fur Seal Treaty of l9ll: the number of American
fur seals might be reduced to 135,000, which was the
number in l9ll, whereas under the protection of the Treaty
the number is now approximately 1,250,000. (The Japanese
have in the past expressed a desire for a larger share
of the proceeds from the sale of the skins - now 15 per
cent - and a desire that the number of seals be reduced.)
Denunciation of the Treaty would cause a loss in revenue
to the Government: since 191l about 500,000 skins have
been taken. The dressing and dyeing industry which has been
built up in St. Louis as a result of the convention
would be destroyed, affecting the livelihood of several
hundred people, and it could be anticipated that this
industry would again center in London as it did before
the industry was built up at St. Louis. Refusal of
permission to the HAKUHO MARU, in case it annoyed the
Japanese, might result in the Japanese not confining
their fishing activities in the Alaskan waters to the
taking of crabs, which might mean that the American
salmon industry would be very seriously impaired with a
loss of possibly a good many millions of dollars a year.
In case the Japanese were to denounce the Fur Seal
Treaty, the United States would lose a possible way of
dissuading

=== Page 245 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
dissuading the Japanese from interfering with the salmon
runs in Alaskan waters, namely, the United States would be
unable to offer revision of the Treaty as a compensation
to the Japanese for not fishing salmon. Mr. Bower also
stated that with regard to questions releting to the fur
seal industry and to the crab industry conducted in Alaskan
waters by Japanese interests we have made no concessions
to the Japanese points of view.
When Mr. Salisbury suggested to Mr. Bower that the
HAKUHO MARU might be granted permission to put in only at
those ports in the Aleutian Islands where there are Ameri-
can officials - visits at other points being regarded as
undesirable for the reason that the records indicate that
such visits have disturbed the natives - Mr. Bower stated
(substantiating his statement by perusal of the Annual Re-
port of the Governor of Alaska for the year ending June 30,
1932) that there is only one place in the Aleutian Islands
where there are American officials, namely, Unalaska (which
is sometimes referred to as Dutch Harbor as Dutch Harbor
is only one mile from Unalaska). He further stated that
in his belief there were only three places in the Aleutian
Islands where there are native inhabitants, namely,
Unalaska Island, Umnak Island and Attu Island. (Between
these last two named Islands there is a distance of some
500 or 600 miles.)
Mr. Bower

一4-
Mr. Bower concluded with the statement that, in his
opinion, it would be regrettable to refuse permission
to the HAKUHO MARU in view of the possible effects which
it might have on the fur seal industry and on the American
salmon industry.

MEMORANDUM FOR COMMANDER H. M. LAMMERS, OFFICE OF NAVAL O ERATIONS,
F/HS
NAVY DEPARTMENT.
102.16
S
In connection with your oral suggestion of the 22nd
instant, you are advised that the following named vessels of the
Coast Guard will comprise the Bering Sea Patrol Force during the
894.62
ensuing summer:
Coast Guard cutter TAHOE, which leaves San Fran-
cisco on 10 June, remaining in Bering Sea until ap-
proximately 16 September;
628-VESSELS/79
Coast Guard cutter SHOSHONE, which left San Fran-
do
cisco l0 April, and will remain in Bering Sea until
approximately 27 June;
Coast Guard cutter CHELAN, which leaves Seattle,
Wash., 25 June and will remain in Bering Sea until ap-
proximately 2l September;
Coast Guard cutter ALERT, which loft Ketchikan,
Alaska, 15 April and will remain in Bering Sea until
approximately 2l September.
In addition to the above listed vessels, the Coast
Guard cutter NORTHLAND will make her usual cruise to the Arctic,
passing en route through Bering Sea and basing at Nome, Alaska,
during the greater part of the summer months.
In further connection with this subject, your attention
is invited to the fact that the patrol of the Bering Sea for the
purpose of enforcing pelagic sealing laws is a duty that has been
assigned to the Coast Guard for a long period of years and, quite
naturally, personnel engaged in this work are perhaps better fitted,
through experience, to handle the problems arising therewith than
FILED
any other class of Government officers.
5
8
The Navy Department has been advised fully within the
3
past few months relative to the activities of Japanese fishing
craft in the waters of the Bering Sea, and it is believed that this
phase of the situation requires no further illumination. Prior to

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.99)
19l4, the Japanese Government frequently assigned a vessel
of the Japanese Navy, usually of the gunboat class, to duty
in Bering Sea, following the policy of the English and Russian
Governments in this regard. The vessel so assigned always
based on Unalaska, and so far as has been noted, did not base
at any other Bering Sea ports during her cruise. Vessels of
this category remain nover longer than a period of two or three
weeks in Bering Sea.
In view of the possibilities of a Government despatched
vessel visiting Bering Sea waters during the ensuing summer, and
if this vessel be entitled to such courtesies, the Coast Guard
will be very glad to extend such formal courtesies, as may be
indicated in the premises. Further, if the Navy Department so
indicates, the Coast Guard will detail a particular vessel of
the Bering Sea Patrol Force, or additional vessels, if necessary,
to accompany the Japanese vessel concerned while the latter is
cruising in Bering Sea. It is believed that if this courtesy were
extended to the Japanese, it would be a helpful act of comity be-
cause the ofricers of the Coast Guard are particularly familiar
with the pilotage, etc. in those waters.
(Signed) H. G. Hamlet,
Rear Admiral, U. S. Coast Guard,
Commandant.

Abrogation of the treaty would result in a return of the

Any action which micht occasio. at rogation  treaty also
in all probability would result in the destruetion of the  erican

In the sealin: season of 193z over 49,0c0 fur-seal skins were

In any consideration of action affecting the visits of Japan-

fast intention to refrain from any activities in Bering Sea
waters that would result in the canture of salmon.
There is no doubt whatever that, should the Japanese

My dear Mr. Secretary:
F/HS
