Acknowledgment is made of State Department letter of 19 May,
file  894.628/100, transmitting copy of a note from the Japanese
S
Imbassy under date of 9 May stating that the HAkUHO MARU, of the
Japanese Department of Agriculture and Forestry, will leave Japan
894.628-VESS
some time in June or July to cruise in the vicinity of the Aleutian
Islands for the purpose of investigating the migration route of fur
894.
seals and "supervising" poaching activities there, and requesting
the usual courtesies and facilities in case it may be necessary for
the vessel to call at the ports of those islands to secure shelter
and provisions.
From the point of view of this department, it is contrary to
22
the interests of national defense for this government to permit
Japanese public vessels to visit the Aleutian Islands excopt at
ports where there are government officials. It is understood that
S
the only port in this category at the present time is Unalaska.
ELS
The Aleutian Islands, like the Japanese Mandate Islands, are
S
potentially of great strategic importance in case of war in the
Pacific. Complete naval intelligence of the hydrography and other
1
8
features of the Aleutian Islands might be of great advantage to
Japan in case of war, in the same manner that it would be of material
benefit to the United States to extend our present imperfect knowledge
of the Mandate Islands. The Japanese Government has not hesitated to
guard most jealously all possible naval information with regard to its
possessions in question, by failing to publish to the maritime world the
results of its surveys in those islands, and by consistently refusing
U
to permit the visits thereto of foreign public vessels except at certain
pecified ports where it maintains government officials. In the opinion
10
of this department, it is of material importance for this government
6
similarly to guard the information of naval interest with respect to the
9
3
Aloutian Islands.
From a recent conference between representatives of our respective
departments and the Department of Commerce it is understood that the
latter dopartment is inclined to the view that under Article VIII of the
l9ll Convention for the Protection and Preservation of the Fur Seals and
Sea Otter the United States is obligated to facilitate the proposed
visits of the HAkUHO MARU to points in the Aloutian Islands, and that
independently of any question of treaty obligation that department is
apprehensive lest a refusal on our part wi th respect to these visits
may result in a Japanese denunciation of this convention, with un-
fortunate effects upon the fur seal and fisheries industries in the

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.98)
Bering Sea area. While the Navy Department would be loath to recommend
the violation of a treaty obligation, it does not so far perceive that
such an obligation exists in connection with the proposed visits now in
question. Article I of the convention provides, in effect, that the duly
commissioned officers of any of the high contracting powers may seize or
detain the nationals or vessels of any of the parties for offenses against
the prohibition with respect to pelagic sealing, delivering them as soon
as practicable to an muthorized official of their own nation; but specific-
ally provides that the officers of one power are not empowered to make such
an arrest within the territorial jurisdiction of another power. Article
VII imposes upon the United States, Japan, and Russia, respectively, the
obligation to maintain a guard or patrol in the waters frequented by the
seal herd in the protection of which it is especially interested. From
these articles it would seem clear in principle that the obligations
imposed upon each power are applicable only within its own jurisdiction
and the waters adjacent thereto, and that no power has received by this
convention any obligation or corresponding rights applicable within the
territorial jurisdiction of another power. So far as this department is
able to perceive,: the convention imposes no obligation upon the Japanese
Government to conduct any operations within United States territorial
waters, either of police duty or of scientific investigation, and there-
fore imposes upon the United States no obligation to facilitate or permit
such operations.
With respect to the necessity for visits to Aleutian Islands harbors
in case the HAkUHO MARU pursues its intended voyage, it would not appear
that such visits will be necessary for the announced purpose of securing
provisions. It is a reasonable assumption that in accordance with usual
modern practice in sea-going vessels the HAKUHO MARU is equipped with a
distilling apparatus which will render it independent of the shore in
the matter of fresh water, and so far as this department is aware there
are no stocks of provisions available at any point in the Aleutian Islands
other than Unalaska, and even there in very limited quantities.
Apart from the question of treaty obligation, this department is
mindful of the views entertained by the Department of Commerce with
respect to the possible adverse affect in case Japan should denounce
the present fur seal convention, and would be glad to find a solution
which will reconcile the apparently conflicting interests of national
defonse and the fur seal and fisheries industries.
It is understood that the Coast Guard is prepared, if necessary,
to assign a cutter to accompany the HAkUHO MARU on its proposed cruise
in Alaskan waters. In case it is deemed necessary or advisable, either
because of treaty obligation or as a matter of policy to sanction the
visits of this Japanese vessel at points in the Aleutian Islands other
than Unalaska, it would appear that the assignment of such a United
States vessel to accompany it would constitute a full measure of
Oonfidential
- 2 -

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.95)
cooperation and courtesy, and will at the same time sufficiently
meet the objections which this department otherwise holds.
Sincerely yours,
100
Honorable Cordell Hull,
Secretary of State.

=== Page 287 === (confidence 0.99)

IMPERIAL JAPANESE CONSULATE
CENTRAL BUILDING
SEATTLE
May 3, 1933.
Commandant, Bering Sea Patrol Force,
United States Coast Guard,
Unalaska, Alaska.
Dear Sir :
I wish to inform you that, through my Government, I have
received a communication from the President of the Government ishery
Institute of Tokyo, Japan, stat ing that a training vessels of that
institute, the Hakuyo Laru, tonnage 1,327 and equipped with a Diesel
engine, is to make its eighth cruise for the purpose of ins tructing
its students in pelagic fishery and also for the purpose of afford-
ing them practice in navigation and seamanship.
The ship will be commanded by Captain Jinzo Nakagawa and
will sail with a group of graduating seniors numbering 32 and also
with a crew of 44 men, includ ing 15 officers. They will leave Japan
on May 14th, and as shown in the enclosed schedule, will arrive at
Dutch Harbor, Unalaska on July 13th to remain there until July 17th.
They will subsequently visit Saint raul, Pribilof Islands, arriving
there on August 1lth, to remain there until August 14th. At both
these points they wish to land in order to make a study tour of
these places. I am enclosing herewith the schedule of the training
ship and also other data for your information.
In this connection, the Institute and the personnel of
its training ship, as well a myself, will highly appreciate any
courtesies and facilities as may be possible extended to the offi-
cers, students and the cre" of tho Hakuyo Maru with reference to
their visits to the above mentioned places in Alaska and also during
their cruise in Alaskan waters. It is further requested that in any
case of emergency the necessary attention and protection be given
them by the proper authorities concerned.
Respectfully yours,
(Signed) KIYOSHI UCHIYAMA
Consul for Japan.

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.99)

Apr11 3, 1934.
After conferring with Mr. Patterson of
PC, I telephoned to Commander Train and
informed him of the receipt by this Department
of Tokyo's telegram of March 28, 1 p. m., in
regard to the prospective visit to Dutch
Harbor and St. Paul of the Japanese training
ship HAKUYO MARU. I referred to correspondence
in 1933 between this Department and the Navy
Department in regard to the visits of Japanese
public vessels to ports in the Pribilof
Islands. I referred particularly to the Navy
Department's letter of June 7, 1933, wherein
it was stated that there would be no objection
on the part of the Navy Department to visits
of Japanese public vessels at ports in the
Aleutian Islands area where officials of this
Government are stationed. I said that as it
appeared that American officials were
stationed at Dutch Harbor and St. Paul we
wondered whether the Navy Department would
interpose objection to the HAKUYO MARU
visiting those ports as planned.
After looking up the Navy file,
Commander Train telephoned on April 3 and
said that the attitude of the Navy epartment
would be that set forth in its letter of
June 7 and that there would be no objection
to this public vessel visiting the ports of
Dutch Harbor and St. Paul. I told him that
this Department would therefore send a tele-
gram to Tokyo suggesting that request for
permission

=== Page 289 === (confidence 0.95)

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
permission for the visits of the Japanese
vessel in question be made through the usual
diplomatic ohannels; and that, upon receipt
of such request through diplomatic channels,
this Department would refer the matter in the
usual way to the Navy Department.

neeessary for the ship to visit the above mentioned places

6

3

Acknowledgmont is made of State Department letter of 9 May,
file PX 894.628-Vessels/96, concerning the proposed visit of the
Japanese Scouting ship HAKUHO MARU of the Department of Agriculture
and Forestry at certain ports in the Aleutian lslands.
As it is understood that there are officials of the United
894.628-VESSELS/104
States Government at the ports which the Japanese public vessel in
question proposes to visit, this department cannot properly offer
any objection to those visits. However, in view of the strategic
importance of the Aleutian Islands this department considers it
most undesirable that Japanese public vessels be permitted to
cruise freely in that area unless and until the Japanese Govern-
ment evinces a willingness to grant to this government reciprocal
privileges in Japanese controlled areas, particularly the Mandate
Islands of the Pacific.
In this connection it will be recalled that for a number of
years, beginning in l92l, it has been the desire of this department
to have naval vessels visit ports in the Mandated Islands. On each
occasion of a proposed visit of naval vessels to ports in those
islands the Department of State has made informal inquiry of the
Japanese Foreign Office and has been informed that while the Japanese
Government would be very glad to have United States naval vessels pay
visits at any of the open ports in the Mandate Islands thoy could not
see their way clear to welcoming visits to unopened ports or out of
the way places because harbor accommodations were limited, there were
no pilots available, and these harbors and anchorages were difficult
of approach and at times dangerous. The Japanese have on occasions
named the open ports, the inference being that all other ports in the
mandated islands were closed ports. In a letter to your department on
13 July, 1929, the Secretary of the Navy invited attention to the treaty
8113394/104
tipulations bearing on the subject and concluded that the Navy Depart-
ment believes that Japan has no right, under the treaty, to prohibit
the entry of American vessels in any of the harbors of the Mandate
NOV 3 0 1934
Islands, and further stated that it was desired from time to time,
FILED
as our vessels travel from one station to another between the West
Coast of the United States and the Far East, to take advantage of the
opportunity to obtain hydrographic information of the islands.
This department was informed in State Department note, file E
811.3594/132, of 25 January, 1930, of an interview which the American
Charg d'Affaires ad interim at Tokyo had with the Vice Minister for

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.98)
Foreign Affairs on the subject of visits of our naval vessels to Japanese
Mandated Islands in which the Vice Minister told the Charge that the Overseas
Dopartment had finelly come to the conclusion to abide by whatever decisions
the Navy Department made in this matter. The Japanese Navy, he said, had
informed the Department of Foreign Affairs that they would raise no objection
whatever to visits by our men-of-war to those of the islands where Japanese
officials were present. He further stated that the Navy would be glad if
we would not ask to visit those islands where there are no Japanese officials
because such visits were disturbing. The Vice Minister suggested that when-
over our men-of-war intended to visit any of the islands we inform the Japanese
Government beforehand and that the Navy Department would then indicate what
islands were open. The Charge stated that the Japanese have a feeling that
it would be impolite on their part not to have officials present to welcome
visiting foreign men-of-war.
811.3397/137
On 3 November, 1930, this department requested the Secretary of State
to obtain Japanese assent to visits of the U.S.S. HOUSTON to Wotje, Wolea
and Anguar early in l93l, during the passage of that vessel to the Asiatic
Station. The Embassy at Tokyo was instructed by telegram to take this
matter up informally with the Minister of Foreign Affairs before presenting
a formal request. The Department of State deemed this procedure advisable
at the time as the question of such visits to the Mandated Islands had
recently been under discussion with the Japanese Government and no satisfactory
precedent had been established. On 6 December, l930, the State Department in-
811.339
formed this department that the Japanese Foreign Office had sent an informal
1-19
notification to the American Embassy stating that purely for reasons of internal
civil administration in the Mandate Islands the departments concerned enter
tained the hope that the HoUSTON would visit neither Wotje nor Wolea but that
there was not the slightest objection to her calling at open ports. On 18
152
December, l930, this department informed the Department of State that plans
for the passage of the HOUSTON to the Asiatic Station had been changed and that
no visit to the Mandated Islands would be included in her itinerary, but that
tho U.S.S. RAMAPO, U.S.S. HART, and the U.S.S. RIZAL were due to return to the
United States from the Asiatic Station and this department desired to have them
visit Wotje, nearest island they would pass, to fuel. On 20 December, 1930, the
1154
State Department acknowledged receipt of this department's letter and stated
that in view of the correspondence with respect to the proposed visit of the
HOUSTON, the State Department felt the time inopportune to ask concerning a
visit of other vessels to Wotje and further stated that it was still of the
view that this matter could best be worked out by friendly approaches, at
intervals, on the part of the Embassy to the Japanese Foreign Office.
In view of the fact that Japanese public vessels have been permitted to
cruise freely in the Aleutian Islands area, which comprehends United States
territory and territorial waters, it is requested that this department be
informed as to whether or not the Department of State is prepared to make
formal notification to the Japanese Government of a proposed visit of United
- 2 -

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.96)
States naval vessels to ports in the former German islands in the Pacific
Ocean, north of the Equator, which are mandated to the Government of Japan,
and concerning which the United States and Japan ratified a treaty in l922
regarding the rights in those islands of the two governments and their
respective nationals.
Respectfully,
MHandluy

After I had drafted this note to Navy,
DCR brought to my attention the Solicitor's
memorandum of August 21, 1929, and memorandum
of conversation on September 27, 1929,
between Admiral Standley and officers of this
divis on, on the question of the right of
American naval vessels to enter any port
in the Japanese Mandated Islands.
The legal aspects of the question have,
therefore, been orally explained to the
Navy Department, but as the Navy Department
has not taken cognizance of them it would
seem necessary to do so byletter.
We apparently do not have a copy of the
bas:o law of Japan relating to open ports,
but Article I of the Harbor Regulations
for Open Ports states: "The limits of the
undermentioned ports open to foreign commerce
are as follows". The open ports in the
Mandated Islands are set forth in the
annual reports of the Japanese Government
to the League.

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
WASHINGTON
In reply refer to
894.628-Vesse1s/104
The Honorable
The Secretary of the Navy.
Sir:
Reference is made to the Navy Department's letter,
dated May 21, 1934 (A4-5(1)(330413-1), 1n the final para-
graph of which the request is made that the Navy Department
be informed whether this Department is prepared to give
formal notification to the Japanese Government of a proposed
visit of United States naval vessels to ports in the Japanese
Mandated Islands.
The Department has given careful and sympathetic con-
sideration to the views of the Navy Department as set forth
in the letter under acknowledgment, and to the treaties and
Senate resolutions bearing on the subject of your letter
cited in the Navy Department's letter of July 13, 1929
(EF37/A4-3(29019). Although the provisions of the various
resolutions, treaties and notes cited by the Navy Department
operate to insure to American nationals such rights and privi-
leges in the Mandated Islands as may be possessed or acquired
by citizens and governments of other foreign countries, it
is the opinion of this Department that the assertion
of

=== Page 346 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
appropriate representation to the Japanese Government
with a view to the granting by Japan in this connec-
tion of the "usual comity" which it has undertaken
to extend to vessels of the United States.
The records of the Department show that on
September 27, 1929, legal and diplomatic features of
this problem were discussed at length between Admiral
Standlev, then Assistant to Chief of Naval Operations,
Captain William R. Furlong, and officers of the Depart-
ment.
Since the receipt of your letter under acknowledg-
ment, the Department has, as stated earlier in this
letter, given the matter further consideration, and
its views with regard to the legal aspects are as
stated above. To the diplomatic aspects, the Depart-
ment has at intervals given thought and will continue
to give thought.
Very truly yours,

=== Page 347 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
"The citizens or subjects of each of the
Contracting Parties ...... shall have liberty
freely to come with their ships and cargoes to
all places, ports and rivers in the territories
of the other which are or may be opened to
foreign commerce, subject always to the laws of
the country to which they thus come".
It may, therefore, be assumed that if the American
Government were to assert a claim to the right of its pub-
lic vessels to enter unrestrictedly all ports in the Mandated
Islands, the Japanese Government would reply in effect that:
(a) The Mandate, in the validity of which the United States
has acquiesced, authorizes Japan to apply Japanese laws to
the Mandated Islands; (b) the Japanese law, restricting the
entry of foreign vessels only to certain designated ports in
Japan, has been extended to the Mandated Islands; (c) the
Treaty of Commerce and Navigation between the United States
and Japan restricts the right of entry of American vessels to
open ports in Japan and, as the United States has acquiesced
in the application of existing treaties between the United
States and Japan to the Mandated Islands, to open ports in
the Mandated Islands.
There would, therefore, appear to be inadequate legal
basis for contesting the action of the Japanese Government
in refusing to permit American public vessels to enter
closed ports in the Mandated Islands. If, however, any
evidence is available that vessels of other foreign nations
have been permitted to enter such ports, this Department
would be prepared to consider making, at a convenient moment,
appropriate

=== Page 348 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
of the claim that American public vessels are entitled freely
to enter all of the ports of the Mandated Islands would elicit
a reply some points in which it would be difficult to contro-
vert.
In the so-called Treaty of Yap, concluded between the
United States and Japan on February 11, 1922, the United
States consented to the administration by Japan, pursuant to
the Mandate conferred upon the Emperor of Japan, of all the
former German islands in the Pacific Ocean lying north of
the Equator. Article II of that Mandate states:
"The Mandatory shall have full power of ad-
ministration and legislation over the territory
subject to the present Mandate as an integral por-
tion of the Empire of Japan, and may apply the laws
of the Empire of Japan to the territory, subject to
such local modifications as circumstances may require".
It is understood that, under the above-quoted provision
of the Mandate, the Japanese law which forbids the entry by
foreign vessels into ports other than those especially de-
signated as open ports has been made applicable mutatis
mutandis to the Mandated Islands. It will be noted from
paragraph (3), Article II of the above-mentioned treaty
that "Existing treaties between the United States and Japan
shall be applicable to the mandated islands". Article IV of
the Treaty of Commerce and Navigation between the United
States and Japan, signed at Washington on February 21, 19ll,
provides that:
"The

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.98)
BCR
FE/H
In reply refer to
894.688-Vesse1s/104
The Honorable
The Secretary of the Navy.
Sir:
Reference is made to the Navy Department's letter,
dated May 21, 1934 (A4-5(1)(330413-1), 1n the final para-
graph of which the request is made that the Navy Department
be informed whether this Department is prepared to give
formal notification to the Japanese Government of a proposed
visit of United States naval vessels to ports in the Japanese
Mandated Islands.
The Department has given careful and sympathetic con-
sideration to the views of the Navy Department as set forth
in the letter under acknowledgment, and to the treaties and
Senate resolutions bearing on the subject of your letter
cited in the Navy Department's letter of July 13, 1929
(EF37/A4-3(29019). Although the prov1s1ons of the various
resolutions, treaties and notes cited by the Navy Department
operate to insure to American nationals such rights and privi-
legee in the Mandated Islands as may be possessed or acquired
by citizens and governments of other foreign countries, it
is the opinion of this Department that the assertion

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.98)
一2-
of the claim that American public vessels are entitled freely
to enter all of the ports of the Mandated Islands would elieit
a reply some points in which it would be difficult to contro-
vort.
In the so-called Treaty of Yap, concluded between the
United States and Japan on February 11, 1922, the United
States consented to the administration by Japan, pursuant to
the Mandate conferred upon the Emperor of Japan, of all the
former German islands in the Pacific Ocean lying north of
the Equator. Article II of that Mandate states:
"The Mandatory shall have full power of ad-
ministration and legislation over the territory
subject to the present Mandate as an integral por-
tion of the Empire of Japan, and may apply the laws
of the Empire of Japan to the territory, subject to
such local modifications as circumstances may require".
It is understood that, under the above-quoted provision
of the Mandate, the Japanese law which forbids the entry by
foreign vessels into ports other than those especially de-
signated as open ports has been made applicable mutatis
mutandis to the Mandated Islands. It will be noted from
paragraph (3), Article II of the above-mentioned treaty
that "Existing treaties between the United States and Japan
shall be applicable to the mandated islands". Article IV of
the Treaty of Commerce and Navigation between the United
States and Japan, signed at Washington on February 21, 19ll,
provides that:
Tho

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.99)
"The eitizens or subjects of each of the
Contracting Parties ...... shall have liberty
freely to come with their ships and cargoes to
all places, ports and rivers in the territories
of the other which are or may be opened to
foreign commerce, subject always to the laws of
the country to which they thus come".
It may, therefore, be assumed that if the American
Government were to assert a claim to the right of its pub-
lic vessels to enter unrestrictedly all ports in the Mandated
Islands, the Japanese Government would reply in effect that:
(a) The Mandate, in the validity of which the United States
has acquiesced, authorizes Japan to apply Japanese laws to
the Mandated Islands; (b) the Japanese law, restricting the
entry of foreign vessels only to certain designated ports in
Japan, has been extended to the Mandated Islands; (c) the
Treaty of Commerce and Navigation between the United States
and Japan restricts the right of entry of American vessels to
open ports in Japan and, as the United States has acquiesced
in the application of existing treaties between the United
States and Japan to the Mandated Islands, to open ports in
the Mandated Islands.
There would, therefore, appear to be inadequate legal
basis for contesting the action of the Japanese Government
in refusing to permit American publio vessels to enter
closed ports in the Mandated Islands. If, however, any
evidenee is available that vessels of other foreign nations
have been permitted to enter such ports, this Department
would be prepared to consider making, at a convenient moment,
appropriato

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.94)
appropriate representation to the Japanese Govermment with
a view to the granting by Japan in this conneetion of the
"usual comity" whioh it has undertaken to extend to vessels
of the United States.
The records of the Department show that on September 27,
1929, legal and diplomatic features of this problem were dis-
cussed at length between Admiral Standley, then Assistant
to Chief of Naval Operations, Captain William R. Furlong. and
officers of the Department.
Since the receipt of your letter under acknowledgment,
the Department has, as stated earlier in this letter, given
the matter further consideration, and its views with regard
to the legal aspects are as stated above. To the diplomatic
aspects, the Department has at intervals given thought and
will continue to give thought.
Very truly yours,

area at a time when a naval au rveying expedition and other vessels of
the United States Fleet are operatine in that area for the purpose of
collecting data which may be used as the basis of a study ith a view
to requestine that an executive order be issued dec. arin that certain
areas in the Aleutians be closed to foreicn entry.

under consideration are, regurdless of the officially declured mission,
partly for the purpose or eathering  ilitary inforaction. The activities

be cl sed to foreign ships.
On the occa. ion of the visit of the United States destroyers POPE
and FORD to the Kurile Islands to act as plane guards for the United
States Army Around the orld Flight in 1924 the Japanese Government,
as an act of courtesy, placed on board each destroyer a Japanese n val

to accompany each of the United States destroyers durin th time that

m

A

main on board so long as the vessel remains in such waters.
We perceive no objection to the Navy Department inform-

may be operating in that area would not be convenient; that
mevertheless no objestion will be interposed to the proposed

DIVISION OF
Draft Bill to Regulate Japanese Fishing
Vessels Operating in American Waters.
894.623/1
Yesterday, January 25, 1935, Major Chipman sent me
894.628
the final draft of the bill drawn up by the interdepart-
mental committee (War, Navy and Commerce) which the
President set up to consider ways and means of regulating
6
Japanese fishing vessels operating in American waters.
Major Chipman said that the committee is anxious to
submit the bill to the President and requested that I
study the bill and let him know today whether it would
be satisfactory to this Department.
I informed Major Chipman by telephone today that
the purport of the bill appeared to be entirely
satisfactory. Major Chipman then suggested that it would
be helpful if the Department formally notified the War
F/FG.
Department by letter to that effect. I reminded Major
Chipman of the fact that the Department is not represented
on the interdepartmental committee. Major Chipman recalled
AN2
that such was the case. He added that the committee would
inform the President, in submitting the draft bill, that the
Department of State had been informally consulted in the matter.

CONVERSATION:
Major Chipman,
War Department General Staff.
Mr. Dooman.
Subject:
Draft Bill to Rogulato Japanese Fishing
Vessels Operating in American Waters.
Yesterday, January 25, 1935, Major Chipman sent me
the final draft of the bill drawn up by the interdepart-
mental committee (War, Navy and ommerce) which the
President set up to consider ways and means of regulating
Japanese fishing vessels operating in Amorican waters.
Mejor Chipman said that the committee is anxious to
submit the bill to the resident and requested that I
study the bill and let him know today whether it would
be satisfactory to this Department.
I informed Major Chipman by tele hone today that
the purport of the bill arpeared to be entirely
satisfactory. Major Chipman then suggested t) et it would
be helpful if the Department formally notified the ar
Department by letter to that effect. I reminded Major
Chipman of the fact that the Department is not represented
on the interdepartmental committee. Major Chipman recalled
that such was the case. He added that the committee would
inform the President, in submitting the draft bill, that the
Department of State had been informally consulted in the matter.

=== Page 410 === (confidence 0.99)
B I L L
To regulate vessels in the fisheries, and for other purposes.
Be it enacted by the Senate and House of Representatives of the
United States of America in Congress assembled, That no vessel owned
in whole or in part by any person who is not a citizen of the United
States shall, under penalty of forfeiture, be operated or navigated
on any waters within the territorial jurisdiction of the United States,
its territories or its possessions, unless such vessel is provided with
and has on board a register, or other document of like import in lieu
thereof, issued to it under and in accordance with the laws of a foreign
country whose government is recognized by the United States and with
which we have commercial relations, and is also provided with and has on
board a descriptive list of the entire crew showing their rating, nation-
ality and residence, and a descriptive list of all others on board show-
ing their nationality, residence and occupation or business, also a mani-
fest of all cargo on board; Provided, that the terms "person" and "citi-
zen of the United States" as used in this act shall have the meaning as-
signed to them by sections 1 and 2 of the Shipping Act, 1916 as amended
by the Merchant Marine Act of 1920; and Provided further, that this sec-
tion shall not apply to vessels not exceeding 26 feet in length, measured
from end to end over the deck, excluding sheer.
SEC. 2. That any officer of the United States authorized to board
vessels for the purpose of enforcing the revenue, navigation and/or immi-
gration laws of the United States, and/or any officer of the United States
Navy, is hereby authorized and empowered to board any vessel within the

=== Page 411 === (confidence 0.99)
provisions of this act and to make such examination of the vessel, per-
sons and cargo on board, and all papers and/or documents relating or per-
taining thereto which are required by this act, as may be necessary to
carry out the purpose and intent thereof. If the master or person in
charge or command of such vessel fails or refuses to deliver any list,
manifest, or other document required by this act to be on board such ves-
sel on demand of any officer herein authorized to examine the same, he
shall be liable to a fine of not to exceed $200 for which fine the vessel
shall be liable. If any person or persons obstruct or resist any author-
ized officer in the discharge of his duties under this act, or aid, abet,
or assist in such obstruction or resistance, such person or versons shall
be deemed guilty of a misdeneanor and shall, on conviction thereof, be
subject to a fine of not more than $1,Oo0 or to imprisonment for not more
than two years, or to both such fine and imprisonment, at the discretion
of the court; Provided, that any officer authorized by this act to enforce
its provisions may arrest, or, in the case of attempted escape, may pursue
and arrest any person or persons who obstruct or resist such officer in
the performance of his duties, or shall aid, abet, or assist in such ob-
struction or resistence; and Provided, that any person or persons so ar-
rested shall be taken forthwith before the nearest Commissioner of a Dis-
trict Court of the United States who, if the evidence warrants such action,
shall hold such person or persons to answer and stand trial before the
Court of the United States having jurisdiction of the offense; and Provided
further, that nothing in this section shall be construed as repealing
section 4336 of the Revised Statutes.
SEC. 3. That any vessel. not a vessel of the United States, engaging

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.99)
In the fisheries, shall be subject to forfeiture; Provided, that vessels
of less than five net tons wholly owned, operated, and navigated by citi-
zens of the United States, and which are numbered in accordance with the
act of June 7, 1918, shall be deemed vessels of the United States for the
purposes of this act.
SEC. 4. That any vessel owned wholly or in part by a person not a
citizen of the United States and engaged in fishing on the high seas or in
foreign waters, shall, on arriving at or departing from a port of the
United States, its territories and/or insular possessions, make formal
entry, and on leaving such port, make formal clearance under the same
conditions, regulations, and penalties as apply to vessels engaged in trade
with foreign countries; Provided, that unless such vessel enters the
United States from a foreign port, it shall not be required to pay tonnage
dues or other fees not collectitle from vessels of the United States
under the same circumstanoes and conditions.
SEC. 5. That nothing in this act shall be construed as being in
contravention of the provisions of any existing treaty, convention, or
agreement between the United States and any foreign Government.
SEC. 6. That any vessel subject to forfeiture nder any of the
provisions of this act, or to liability for any other penalty prescribed
herein, may be seized and proceeded against by way of libel in the dis-
trict court of any district in which such vessel may be found.
SEC. 7. Nothing in this act shall be construed as repealing or
limiting any existing law relative to the boarding of vessels.
SEB. 8. The Secretary of Commerce shall make such regulations as

=== Page 413 === (confidence 0.99)
may be necessary to secure the proper enforcement of this act by col-
lectors of customs and other officers of the Government, and may miti-
gate or remit any penalty provided for herein if in his judgment such
action is justified.
SEC. 9. This act shall become effective from and after siz
months from the date of its passage.

THE GAIMUSHO
May 14th, 1935.
TOKIO
Dear Mr. Neville,
The information has been received from the Crab
Floating-Canneries Association that, as in the previous
years, the following vessels left Hakodate in the latter
part of April last for the fishing-grounds in the Bering
Sea, in order to engage in the crab-fishery and allied
operations (Of these vessels, the principal operation on
board the S.S. "Taihoku Maru" is that of making fish-meal):
1. "Ryokai Maru" (4,643 tons)
with accessory motor-boats:
"Aki Maru" (80 tons), "Kasuga Maru" (49 tons)
and "Suiten Maru No. II" (44 tons).
2. "Taihoku Maru" (8,253 tons)
with accessory trawlers:
"Toko Maru", "Hokkai Maru" and "Hakurei Maru"
(of 398 tons each), and also
with accessory "sea-bottom seine" motor-boats:
"Toshikuni Maru", "Chikyu Maru", "Oyo Maru",
"Wafu Maru", "Kashin Maru", "Yoko Maru",
"Toyo Maru" and "Heiwa Maru" (of 88 tons each).
I understand that all the above-mentioned vessels will
operate in the high seas and none of them will enter any
American waters, but venture to transmit to you the preced-
ing information for what interest it may have to you.
Sincerely yours,
Edwin L. Neville, Esq.,
(Signed) S. TOGO
Counsellor,
Director of the Bureau
U. S. Embassy.
of European and West-
Asiatic Affairs.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
Reference is made to your letter of May 21, 1935
[A4-5(1)(350403)], in which it is stated that your Depart-
ment does not interpose objection to the visits of the
HAKUHO MARU, a vessel of the Japanese Department of Agri-
culture and Forestry, to certain harbors in the Aleutian
Islands area.
894.6
In contrast to the proposed visits of the HAKUYO MARU
to Duteh Harbor nd St. Paul Island which formed the subjeet
2
of this Department's letter of April 3, 1935, and of the
8
Navy Department's letter of April 23, 1935, and whioh it
VE
is understood have been abandoned, the proposed visits
ESSELS/1
under reference of the HAKUHO MARU would be made on the basis
of the provisions of the so-called Fur Seals Convention of
l9ll, of which the United States is one of the signatories.
26
In
The Honorable
Clande A. Sua
Seeretary of the Navy.

=== Page 446 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
In view of that fact, it is the opinion of this Department
that any arrangement, such as that proposed by the Navy
Department, for a United States vessel and an American
naval officer to meet the HAKUHO MARU upon her entry into
territorial waters of the United States and to accompany
that vessel during the time that it remains in United States
juriadiction, might be regarded as being not compatible
with purposes of the Fur Seals Convention, especially as
this Government last year gave its assent to the visits of
the mame vessel to Alaskan waters without proffer of
assistance in accomplishing the stated purposes of the
visits. As indicated orally on May 17, 1935, by the Under
Seeretary of State to Captain Townsend of the Navy Depart-
mont, it is the thought of this Department that the Navy
Department may wish to take action in regard to the HAkUHO
MARU similar to that which was taken last year, and that
the visits of that vessel to Alaskan ports be treated as a
routine matter.
It may be added, for the strictly confidential infor-
mation of the Navy Department, that this Department has
responded favorably to a request by the Department of
Commerce that the Japanese Government be approaohed, at
the earliest practicable opportunity, in regard to the
conclusion by the United States and Japan of a treaty which
would

=== Page 447 === (confidence 0.95)
would forbid Japanese nationals from engaging in fishing
operations in the open seas of Bristol Bay. It is the
opinion of the Department of Commerce, in which opinion
this Department concurs, that the conolusion of a treaty
of this character would be highly desirable from the point
of view of protecting the important salmon industry in
Alaska. It is, therefore, considered desirable that no
action be taken by this Government which would predispose
the Japanese Government to view with disfavor the conclusion
of the proposed treaty.
Accordingly, it would be appreciated if the Navy
Department would notify its officers in Alaskan waters
of the contemplated visits of the HAKUIO MARU in the light
of the views of this Department as set forth in the second
paragraph of this letter.
Sincerely yours,
Cordell Hull

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS

MAY 28 1935
41935
My dear Mr. Secretary:
Department of State
The receipt is acknowledged of the Department of State's letter of
May 17, 1935, file PC 711.417/1006, in regard to a note dated May 9, 1955,
from the Japanese Ambassador, in which he states that, in accordance with
Article 10 of the Protocol of the Conference for the Protection of the Fur
Seals in l9ll, the Hakuho Maru will leave Japan on June 6 to cruise in the
vicinity of the Aleutian Islands, touching at Chichagof Harbor, Nazan Bay,
St. Paul Island, St. George Island, and Dutch Harbor, for the purpose of
investigating the migration route of fur seals, and supervising "their
poaching activities" at the Pribilof Islands.
After due consideration of the matter, I wish to advise that the De-
partment of Commerce can see no objection to the proposed visit of the
Hakuho Maru at the places named. In the event that the vessel calls at
St. Paul Island and St. George Island, Pribilof Islands, employees of the
Bureau of Fisheries of this Department will extend the usual courtesies.
The reference in the Japanese Ambassador's note to poaching activities
at the Pribilof Islands is not understood. There is no poaching at these
islands nor, as a matter of fact so far as known, anywhere in the waters
covered by the Fur Seal Convention of l9ll. A strict patrol is maintained
by vessels of the Coast Guard and the Bureau of Fisheries, and it is be-
lieved there is no poaching or unlawful killing of fur seals.
894.628
Another point of interest in the note from the Japanese Anbassador is
the reference to Article 10 of the Fur Seal Convention of 19ll. This ar-
ticle has to do with the delivery of 15 percent of the fur-seal skins taken
at the Pribilof Islands each season to a representative of the Japanese
Government, and a like percentage of skins to the Canadian Government.
During the last two seasons the Canadian Government has elected to
VES
take skins, delivered at Seattle, Washington, instead of 15 percent of the
net proceeds of sale, as had been the case previously since promulgation
of the treaty. The question now occurs as to whether the Japanese Govern-
ment may contemplate taking delivery of skins at the Pribilof Islands this
S
season instead of a proportionate share of the proceeds as heretofore.
E
7
It is hoped that the Japanese Government will continue to have its
N
E
S
1
tional employment in the United States. The Department of Commerce will 
appreciate definite information as to the action planned by the Japanese
191
2
Government in this matter.
3
6
Sincerely yours,
Secretary of Commerce.

The following report from the Commandant of the Eleventh Naval
District, regarding the Japanese Fishery Activities in the Aleutian Islands,
is quoted for your information:
"1. In a personal letter recently received from an officer attached
to the Coast Guard vessel CALYPSo certain obversations were related
concerning the activities of Japanese fisheries in Alaska.
894.628
2. The following extracts from the above-mentioned letter are quoted
for information:
"Our area was a radius of seventy-five miles about the Pribilofs,
(Seal Islands). Found two Japs, anaps enclosed, the little fellow
anchored and the big one underway to beat hell off shore as soon
VESSELS/I
as he saw us. Note the huts built of straw matting and poles fore
and aft. In some places three tiers high. This is one of your
floating canneries with about 1,000 Japs aboard at the time."
"From radio intercepts I gather that there are at present at
least two tenders of the type of the smaller vessel working with
the Ryokai off the Pribilofs. There are also more up in Bristol
Bay and I also note that there is another Jap bound this way with
about eleven trawlers. They stated they are not crab fishermen."
"The first day the officials at St. Paul noticed the Ryokai they
tried to work her by radio. She did not choose to answer so they
3
immediately informed us by radio (we having just arrived at
Unalaska) and shortly afterward our good Jap came back and gave
them a long story about their innocence and that he was the same
one that had been there in previous years."
Very truly yours,

=== Page 464 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
2
DFFARTMENT OF STATE
1935 JUL 30 AM 9 4.9
MEMORANDUM
July 12, 1935.
DIVISION OF
COMMJNICATIONS
ANDS
PROTECTION OF FUR SEALS
8946800
994,628 Venadlo
In a note of May 9, 1935, the Japanese Embassy informs
8
394.62
us that the HAKUHO MARU, of the Department of Agriculture
and Forestry, Tokyo, intends to cruise in the vininity of
2
C
the Aleutian Islands for the purpose of investigating the
VESS
migration route of fur seals.
In this connection, the Japanese Government invokes
ELS
"Article X of the Protocols of the Conference for the Protec-
tion of Fur Seals in 19ll". FE inquires what is meant by
the foregoing citation.
CN
9
The protocols of the International Fur Seal Conference
of l9ll, which are eleven in number, constitute the
minutes or proces-verbaux of the Conference.
22
The following citation from Satow (Diplomatio Practice,
FILED
T
Volume II, page 271): is pertinent:
1935
"During a congress or a conference, no matter
what object or purpose, the minutes of meetings of the
plenipotentiaries are styled either protocol or procos-
verbal, indifferently. Perhaps the former word is the
more dignified. Obviously protocol in this sense does
not mean an agreement; proces-verbal would be better,
as it means that and nothing else".
The

=== Page 465 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
The following excerpts from Paul Fauchille (Volume 3,
page 3l1) is of interest in this connection:
"823. -- Si, au cours de la négociation d'un
traité, l'accord s'établit sur divers points principaux
ou accessoires, il est constaté dans un protocole signé
de toutes les parties. Mais cette signature est
insuffisante à rendre cet accord obligatoire pour les
contractants. Il ne deviendra tel que par la signature
de l'acte final et definitif du traité, constatant que
l'entente s'est formée sur tous les points dont l'ensemble
constitue le traité lui-même".
A final quotation from Charles Cheney Hyde (International
Law, Volume Two, page 40) may be of interest:
"Protocols of meetings are chiefly important as a
means of keeping distinct the several questions
confronting the negotiators, and as thus affording
opportunity to perceive the precise grounds of any
divergence of opinion.In case of subsequent disagree-
ment between the parties, concerning the interpretation
of a treaty, the protocols of the plenipotentiaries
preliminary to its conclusion may shed light respecting
the intention of those individuals as well as of their
governments at the time of negotiation".
From the foregoing citations it should be clear that
the term protocol which constitutes the minutes of a conference
must not be confused with the "same term used to denote -
the form taken by an international compact".
Protocol X reads as follows:
June 30, 1911.
"The International Fur Seal Conference met at the
Department of State at 10 o'clock, with all the Delegates
present.
The Conference discussed the terms of the proposed
Convention and tentatively agreed upon the form and
provisions

=== Page 466 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
provisions thereof, subject to the reservation by the
British Delegation that they were awaiting final
instructions from their Government, which they expected
to receive without delay.
The Japanese proposition that each of the Parties
to this Convention may send from time to time a
scientific expert to the breeding grounds of the others
for the purpose of studying the actual conditions
prevailing thereon and that the said Parties mutually
agree to accord to one another proper facilities in
this respect is in principle accepted by the Conference
as in consonance with the spirit of cooperation on the
part of the respective Powers represented at the
Conference, with the understanding that the visits will
be made at times mutually convenient to the Parties.
Adjournment was then taken subject to the call of
the President."
It should be observed that the Japanese proposal that
the parties to the Convention should from time to time send
a scientific expert to the breeding grounds of the others
was not incorporated in the Convention.
It is my opinion that Protocol X is a minute of the
International Fur Seal Conference which records in writing
but not in the form of a signed undertaking, an agreement
in principle of the delegates to the proposals of one of
the delegations. It has in my mind no binding force and
any refusal to acquiesce to a demand made in virtue of it
would not constitute a violation of the treaty or of any
written undertaking.> But any refusal, I believe, would at
least have to be explained since we acquiesced in principle
to the proposal.
TD:FCDeW:ELN:SS

bear' on Japanese activities in the Bering Sea and as directed by refer
4
ence (a), the following is submitted.
9
2.
On 15 June, 1935, a Japanese of most personable appearance and)
provided with fine pigskin baggage, landed at Unalaska from the S.S. STARR 
and on that day and the two following days was quartered at the Mothodist
Mission. He appeared so furtive and secretive in his efforts to avoid at- <
tontion that I sent him an informal message on the evening of 16 June, re- 
questing him to call on me the following morning. When that morning ar
S
rived he sent a measage stating that he begged to be exoused on aocount of
S
a severe headache whereupon I dispetched to him a military messenger and
directed that he call upon me in my office at 1400, 17 June, 1935, which
7
he finally did.
S
3.
Upon questioning this person, who wms not provided with a visi
ing card, I found his name as indicated on his ashington Motor Vehicle pera
#521164, to be Shigeru Fugii, address Holland Hotel, Seattle, Nashington.
When asked as to his business in Unalaska, he claimed to represent the wife
of a Japanese aviator, one Kiyoshi Homma, who, with two other Japanese av-
iators were lost several years ago in a flight from the Kuril Islands to
Alaska. A rumor had come to Tokyo that this individual was still alive on
the Kenai Peninsula and the wifo of the later, be ing a relat ive of Fugii,
he, Fugii, had ccme to Seward nd False fass to make inquiries relative to
a possible survivor. He clained that he had been supplied with some $300.00
by the widow, which he had expended in this seerch, and the noney now being
gone, he was returning to Seattle via the S.S. VICToRIA, due June 18. Asked
for his passport, he was unable to produce same but alaimed to be a Japanese
student, havin come recently to Seattle from New York and expected to re-
main in this country another year.
4.
While I realize, in view of the Japanese problem, we are inclined
perhaps, to exaggerate the significance of any unexplained activity on the
part, I feal that this story is too perfect. It covers every phase of Fugit's
movements and, at least by we, is unassailable as to truth - the perfect
alibi. His obvious effort to remain under cover for his three days' stay 
and normlly would have mingled about the village, told mo he had found it
very lonesome but preferred to spend his time reading.
5.
In view of our Naval activity in the Bering Sea, this story of
Fugii's should be investigated.
F
H. R. Searles.

Rovomber 4, 1006.
MEMORANDUM
Regulation of "ishing Vessels Owned by
Japanese lationals and Operating
From American orts.
It will be recalled that it was the view of the
Tepartment thet the bill, whict Ied the lorger measure of
su port in the Celiforrie Te islsture, for the control of
fishing vessels owne by Jenenese vationals residing in
the United States and operetiar out of California ports
would, if eracted, probably bo Ield by the courts to he in
conflict with the reaty of lommerce and Wevigation between
the United States and Ja en, ard t' et furt er it "ould
seem to encrcech uron t'e right of t nitec states to
remlete Coreigr nd interstate co merce. Tere were
other ohjections to ti at bill w icr were not expressed
in eny of the officiel papers of the Denartment, ane it
mey be of interest to indicste rere what they were.
1. The bill was apparently prepared from the point
of view of strengthening the nat onal defenses. Effort
was made to build un a case for tre bill from the point
of view of economic and social considerations, but facts
advanced in regard to the number and size of the Japanese
vessels, and the number of persons employed thereon, were
1n

=== Page 483 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
in many cases exaggerated. If, therefore, there is
need for control from military and strategic points of
view of these vessels, basic measures to be taken in ti at
direction sl ould, in my opinion, be formulated and
applied by the Federal Government and not by a state govern-
ment, which should be expected to take measures comple-
mentary to those of the Federal Government.
2. The statement has been made that a considerable
proportion of the Japanese now engaged in fishing turned
to the sea for a livelihood after the passage of California
laws restricting farming by Japanese. If suitable measures
can be devised for the control of fisl ine by Japanese, and
if the assumption is correct that t ese Ja anese are not
displacing native Amorican fish ermen, it would seem
expedient from sccial and economic reasons, as well as
from international political reasons, not to close to
them this field of activity.
3. In view of the large number of American citizens
of Japanese descent who could nominally acquire ownersh'p
of the fishing vessels now owned by Japanese, a law such
as that under consideration this year by the California
Legislature would be difficult of enforcement.
The bill drafted by the Interdepartmental Committee,
requiring that any fishing vessel owned by a foreign
national should be documented by the government of the
country

=== Page 484 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
country to which such national belongs, was a step in
the ri ht direction. If Congress had seen fit to enact
that bill, officers of this Government would have been
in position to exercise a considerable measure of control
over the movements and activities of Japanese fishing
vessels. It is my understanding that certain objections
voiced by members of t! e committee of the ower Louse
to which the bill was referred are being givon considera-
tion and tl:at it is hoped to submit the bill again in amended
form at the next session of Conress.
I am inclined to believe that such measures as those
above referred to do not offer a completely satisfactory
solution of the difficulty -- that they are merely useful
ancillary measures. My thought would be to recommend to
the Navy Department the setting aside as naval reservations of
those areas on the acific Coast -- San Liego, Mare Island,
Bremerton, and others -- which are of essential importance
to our Navy. If the Havy were charged with the control
of all vessels passing in and out of these areas, the
element of danger to the leet would be materially reduced.

=== Page 544 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
m.m.N.
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
May 18, 1936.
CONFIDENTIAL
192
CONVERSATION:
Mr. Dooman.
Subject:
Entry by Japanese Vessels into
Ports in Alaska.
Commander Hill referred to the cases of the Japanese
public vessels HAKUHO MARU and HAKUYO MARU, which are now
394.62
up for consideration by the Navy Department, and he
stated that he had been directed by Admiral Standley
to discuss with some officer in this Department the
questions of future policy with regard to permitting the
VESSELS
entry of Japanese vessels into ports in Alaska, and of
the entry of American public vessels into closed ports in
the Japanese Mandated Islands.
Commander Hill stated that Admiral Standley was
11
generally disposed to agree to the granting of permission
07
9
to the Japanese vessels to enter ports in Alaska, but that
he felt that the question of the entry of American vessels
into closed ports in the Japanese Mandated Islands should
M:Y 2
FII ED
be raised, although not necessarily in connection with the
26 1936
former question.
Mr. Dooman stated that, in his opinion, if the Navy
Department had no strong objection to Japanese vessels
F/FG
entering

=== Page 545 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
entering ports in Alaska, the method of approach which would
be most likely to obtain the consent of the Japanese to the
entry of American public vessels into closed ports in the
South Sea Islands, would be to indicate to the Japanese
Government that this Government will not object to the entry
of Japanese vessels into Alaskan ports, and at the same
time to raise, in some appropriate manner, the question of
the entry of American vessels into ports in the Mandated
Islands.
Commander Hill said that Admiral Standley's thought at
present ran along the lines just indicated by Mr. Dooman.
Commander Hill added, however, that Admiral Standley be-
lieves that some change should be made in the attitude of this
Government with regard to the question of the entry by
Japanese vessels into Alaskan ports after the so-called
"status quo with regard to fortifications in the Pacific"
comes to an end with the termination of the Washington
Treaty. Admiral Standley's thought was that in taking
for the present a very liberal attitude, this Government
should make it clear to the Japanese Government that on
and after January 1, 1937, it will be the policy of this
Government to refuse entry by foreign vessels into closed
ports in Alaska.
Commander Hill presented the question whether there
is any actual difference between the status of so-called
"closed ports" in Japan and ports in Alaska which have not
been

=== Page 546 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
been declared to be "open ports". He said that he had come
to the conclusion, after studying the Treaty of Commerce
with Japan, that Japanese "open ports" were analogous to
American "ports of entry" and that similarly the American
Government may take the same position with regard to those
places where there are no "ports of entry" as the Japanese
Government does with regard to "closed ports". He stated
that it would be probable that this point, together with
other points that might occur to him, would be discussed
in a letter from the Navy Department to the Department.
Commander Hill further stated that the Navy Department
is despatching to Manila during the course of the year a
destroyer replacement, and it was thought that advantage
might be taken of that opportunity to raise the question
of the entry of American public vessels into closed ports
in the South Sea Islands. Mr. Dooman thought that it would
be  referable to prepare the way, by informal discussion
betwen the Department and the Japanese Embassy, for the
raisi s of this question. He suggested that it might be
stressed to the Japanese Ambassador that the American
Government has thus far taken a very liberal attitude with
regard to extending courtesies in Alaskan waters to Japanese
public vessels; that rumors of fortification by Japan of
certain of its South Sea Islands had long been current; and
that the extension by the Japanese Government of similar
courtesies to American public vessels entering ports in the
South Sea Islands would, by dispelling suspicion with regard
to

=== Page 547 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
to Japanese actions in these Islands, tend to improve
relations between the United States and Japan.
Mr. Dooman said that he would recommend to his
superior officers in the Department that such discussion
be had with the Japanese Embassy if and when Admiral
Standley was disposed to indicate that he viewed favor-
ably an approach such as that suggested being made to
the Japanese Government.
Commander Hill replied that he would discuss this
matter vwith Admiral Standley.

628

394.628

This department ha. repeatedly in the past expressed its
objection to unescorted visits of Japanese public vessels to the
sparsely inhabited regions of the leutian Islands. These objections.
are based upon the following facts:
(a) Article IV of the Treaty of Commerce and Navigation concluded
between this country and Japan and signed at ashington D. C.,
21 February, l9ll, reads as follows:
"There shall be between the territories of the two High
Contracting Parties reciprocal freedom of commerce and
navigation. The citizens or subjects of each of the
Contracting Parties, equally with the citizens or subjects
of the most favored nation, shall have liberty freely to
F/FG

=== Page 551 === (confidence 0.99)
come with their ships and cargoes to all places, ports
and rivers in the territories of the other which are or
may be opened to foreign conmerce, subject always to the
laws of the country to which they thus come."
It is the understanding of this department that the "places,
ports and rivers * * * which are or may be open to foreign
commerce" are, under the laws of the United States, only the
ports of entry listed in the Treasury Department publication
(T.D. 48245) of 1 April, 1936. It is noted that in the proposed
1tinerary of the HAKUYO MARU and the HiK HO MARU the following
ports are not listed by the Treasury De artment is ports of
entry:
Kiska
Chichagof Harbor
Bristol Bay
Constantine Bay
St. Paul Island
Nazan Bay
Monterey, Calif.
In addition, Kiska was declared a closed harbor by Executive
Order No. 1613 of 23 September, 1912.
St. Paul Island, however, being a breeding place for fur seals is,
under the Fur Seal Convention of 19ll, and Protocol X thereto,
subject to visit by vessels of the Powers signatory to that treaty
with the understanding that such visits will be ade at times
mutually agreeable to the parties thereto.
(b)
There are no officials present to represent the United States
Government and to safeguard its interest. In this connection
your atte tion is invited to the Sate De urtment note (FE 8ll.
3394/132) of 25 January, 1930, which outlines the attitude of
the Japanese Government regarding visits to Jaralese ports
where there re no officials present.
(c) Entrance by the HAKuHo MaRu into the harhors abeve mentioned is
not re uired "for the purpose of investicating the miration
route of fur seals and of  supervising their poachin uctivities',"
nor are these harbors considered seal "breedine grounds" within
the meaning of Protocol X of the Fur Seal Conference of 19ll.
(a) The Aleutian waters are dang rous to navigate due to lack of
accurate charts, hydrographic infommation regarding tides and
currents, and the abseace of lights, buoys, and other aids to
navigation. hith particular regard to Constantine Bay and Bristol
Bay I wish to call the following facts to your attention:
-2-

=== Page 552 === (confidence 0.98)
The chart of Br stol Bay is of very small seele nd
is not suitable for use in ent ring the territorial
waters of any harbors or bays without considerable
hazard. The shore line is flat and with few distinct
land marks, and at the time in question it is not
believed that there will be qualified pilots available
to place at the di posal of the HLAKUYo MARU.
At Constantine Bay the present chart is based on old
surveys made in 1873. à preliminary exa ination of recent
naval surveys made in this vicinity ha: disclosed the
presence of navigational hazards not indicated on the present
charts and it is, therefore, considered dangerous for vessels
of the size of the HAKUYO MARU to approach or enter Constantine
Bay. This Dep rtment has information to the effect that the
S.S. BROWN B AR, with a Biological Survey party from the
De artment of Agricul ture, Will be in Constantine Bay at about
this time. If so, it would seem advantageous for the HiKUYo
MARU to time her arrival so as to meet the Bho! B.  at this
port; in which case the BReN BiR could furnish navigational
assistance as well as providin the Japanese fisheries experts
with information regardin the sea otter herd which is reported
to exist near that port.
The Washington Naval Treaty expires on 31 December, 1936, and after
that date the status quo of the Aleutian Islands need no longer be maintained.
Although the Navy Department does not contemplate any iamediate development
of any of these harbors as n val bases, it is considered advisable that, com-
mencing 1 January, l937, no further visits of foreign vessels be permitted
to the unopened ports of the Aleutian Islands. s a means of putting such
a ruling into effect, it is suggested that diplomatic notice be given to all
foreign powers that commencing 1 January, l937, visits of foreign vessels,
both public and commercial, will be restricted to ports of entry established
X
by the Treasury Department, or specifically opened by treaty.
Subject to the above comments, and with the understanding that this
will not create a precedent, the Navy Department does not interpose objection
to the proposed visits of the HAKUYO LiARU and the HAKUHO MARU to the ports
listed, including the closed port of Kiska. On the contrary, it is recom-
mended that the Japanese Government be informed that the Navy Department
is pleased to have these vessels visit these ports this year in order that
they may assure themselves that there have been no violations of the ashin ton
Treaty involving the status quo of these islands.
For your personal information, I wish to take this opportunity to
inform you that on about 15 July, 1936, the Navy Department proposes to
-3 -

=== Page 553 === (confidence 0.91)
send a destroyer, the U.S.S. ALDEN, to the Asiatic Station and will,
prior to that time, submit an itinerary for this cruise which will
include visits to certain of tho man ated Islands. Although this
request will not be in any way connected with the al ove mentioned
permission to visit the Aleutian Islands, it is hoped that this whole
question may be presented to the Japanese Government in such a manner
as to secure their favorable consideration of the proposed cruise of
the U.S.S. ALDEN.
Mrandly

The Honorable,
The Secretary of State.

The Tokyo Embassy has been informed
by the Foreign Office that two Japanese
"mother ships", the TOTEN MARU and the

vessels, left Japan in April for
the fishing grounds in the Bering Sea.

operations". The Foreign Office states
that these vessels will not enter American
waters.

to Alaska to investigate the prospects
of open sea fishing . . .", and that
"Hitherto the government has refused
permission to operate in Alaskan waters
because of American fishing interests
. .". The Embassy's report makes no
mention of this vessel.

Subject:
HAKUYO MARU and activities.
Inclosure: (1) Schedule of cruise of HkUYo ARu, 1930.
1.
The HAKUY0 MARU entered Kiska Harbor at O850, 9 June, 1936, and
anchored about five eights of a mile off shore southeast from observation station.
Shortly thereafter the boarding officer from the CYiNE boarded the HAKUYO uRU and
was met at the gangway by the chief officer. le was shown to the saloon and there
was introduced to the captain, J. Nakakawa and two professors of the Tylo Fishing
Institute of the Japanese Imperial Government. He was shown around the ship
generally and ascertained, in general, the followin informtion: The crew proper
was 29 men, 23 cadets, or students of the Tyko Fishing Institute, and 16 officers
and professors. The quarters for the cadets is separate fro that of the officer
or crew. It consisted in generul of staterooms, one for cvery four students, a
chomical laboratory for the study of marine life and a combined study room and mess
hall. Tho HAkUYo MARU is a two masted diesel powered vessel about two hundred
thirty feet in length at the water line, with well deck forward and deck and pilot
house approximately amidships. The vessel is painted vhite all over except for a
band at the top of the dummy stack, which is painted black. The stack is just aft
of the pilot house. The vessel has about a thirty-five foot bean and draft of
between twelve end fifteen feet. The htY0 RU is equipped with two 7oo norsepower
diesel engines, each drivin: a screw, and is capable of makin: approximately 12 knots;
an Aoton (British( yro compass with hre repeuters; electric or hand steering;
sonio depth finder of British make accurate up to 4500 fathoms; a radio direction
finder in the starboard wing of the bridge. The old in the well deck forward con-
tained a small experimental cannery.
2.
 The motor sa1ler of the ILkUyo MU came alon side the CYuE at about
103o towing the CYAnE's surfboat, the boarding officer havin: accepted Captain
Nakakawa's offer to tow them back. Captain Nakakawa and a professor came on board
to call on the commandin officer. Both understood English if spoken slowly, and
could speak it as well, and were able to read and write English. Captain Nakakawa
stated thet he had made several previous cruises from Japan to the Bering Sea and
was familiar with the waters, but had never been in Kiska Harbor before. He stated
that they were using British Admirality charts for the Berine sea. Captein Nakakawa
also tated that the Japanese cannery ships TIhokU MaRU and ToT ARU were fishing
in Bristol Bay and the RYokAI MaRU was crab Tishing off the Komandorsky Islands and
with them would be four trawlers of about 2o0 tons. The professor stated that the
students were making the cruise to get experience in the various types of fishing
encountered in the various places. The captain asked permission for the students
to go ashore for exercise. This permission was granted. The motorboat went ashore
at about 13oo towing a small surfboat in which the men landed on the beach.
The men, after getting on the beack, walked around for a short time and then
climbed the high hill at the head of the bay. They returned to the beach in
about an hour and a half and were picked up by the motorboat and surfbout
and returned to the HAKUYO MARU.

of a mile off shore until reaching the northern end of Kiska Island and
then steamed north northeast until about three miles north of Segula

4.

=== Page 593 === (confidence 0.94)
res
DA
UREAU OF FISHERIES
1931
WASHINGTON
936F 26 AM 49
September 24, 1936.
17
Division of
PON OF
FAR EASTERN ALFAIRS
LONS
Japaneser fishing vessels off Alaskan Coast.
SFP251936
The Secretary of Commerce:
Beginning in 1930 and in the six seasons since then, a mumber of
894.62
Japanese floating crab canneries have operated in high-sea waters a
short distance off the Alaskan Coast, particularly in Bristel Bay.
There has been no indication of salmen canning upon a commercial
basis by any of these vessels. In fact, information received through
the State Department from the American Embassy at Tokyo shows clearly
that the Japanese Government has not licensed any of its vessels to
fish for salmon off the Alaskan Coast.
In the season of 1936 the Hakuvo Maru of the Jepanese Imperial
Fishing Institute visited Bristol Bay and other waters and made ob-
servations on the extent of the sslmon runs. This vessel carries a
number of students who receive training in fishery operations. Also,
the vessel is equipped with laboratory facilities, experimental cen-
ning and by-products mechinery, and scientific apparatus for a thorough
study of deop-sea fishery matters, perticularly from a commercial stand-
point.
2
A few months ago the Japanese Government voted 89,000 yen for a
study of the runs of salmon off the Alaskan Coast. As a result, the
Tenze Marn (657 tons) and accompamying trawler (61 tons) made extensive
studies in Bristol Bay waters in the season of 1936. It is understood
that the investigation thus launched by the Japanese Government 1s to
contime for three seasons.
The Coast Gaard cutter Morris observed that the Japanese vessel
Chichibu Marg had gill mets in operation many miles offshere in Bering
See on Jume 8, 1936, and that salmon were caught. This demomstrates
the feasibility of catching salmon a considerable distance offshore.
As a part of this study, Professor Kamda, of the Imperial Fisher-
1es Sakoel of Makedate, visited Alaske this seasen. In Angast he
attended the Institute ef Pacifie Belations in Tesemite, Califormia,
and speke of his Alaskan investigatiens. He referred te the cordial
treatment given him by representatives of the Buresn of Pisheries.
7

=== Page 594 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Professor Kanda presented the idea that a joint investigation by the
twe governments should be made for the purpose of outlining the policy
and procedure in dealing with the salmon fisheries off the Alaskan
Coast. He indicated that the purpose would be to establish a basis
of negotiations for a treaty to determine the rights of the nations
concerned.
American salmon packers are much alarmed by the possible Japanese
invasion of the valuable Bristol Bay red salmon fishery. The State
Department says that Japanese vessels would be entirely within their
legal right in such operations beyond the three-mile limit.
It is wholly feasible to catch salmon in high-sea waters of
Bristol Bay. As a matter of fact, many of the salmon caught by drift
gill nets of Ameriean fishermen ere taken beyond the three-mile limit
in Bristol Bay. Also, it was demonstrated quite a number of years ago
that purse seines can be operated successfully in these waters. Cur
regulations prohibit seines in the Bristol Bay area, the American
salmon fishery being conducted solely by drift gill nets.
Por a number of years it has been the policy of the State Depart-
ment to avoid any agitation of the question of Japanese vessels fishing
for salmon off the Alaskan Coast. It is understood that the Japanese
Government has been aware of the wish of American interests that there
be no fishing for salmon by Japanese vessels in these waters, but that
the taking of crabs was not objectionable and did not interfere with
Ameriean industry. Thus there has been a more or less of a gentlemen's
agreement for a mumber of years along this line, the Japanese confining
their efforts to crab fishing and the Americans to salmon fishing.
Recently the State Department indicated, informally, that apparent-
ly the time had arrived to let the people of this country know fully
about the impending invasion of our established fisheries in the Bristol
Bay region. The feeling was expressed that a plain statement of facts,
avoiding any spirit of vituperation or animosity, as to the consequences
to American enterprise would present a strong case in letting the people
of this country kmow of the serious situation which confronts a long-
established American business. The effect of any such invasion will be
felt not only by the salmon packing industry, but will have serious
bearing on the business of firms engaged in the manufacture of tin-plate,
cans, fishing gear, and warious items of wide variety used extensively
in the salmen fishory.
Vessels of the Coast Guard have made observations of the operations
of Japanese floating crab canzeries and of Japanese vessels engaged in
investigating the fisheries of Bristol Bay and Bering See this semmer.
The repert from the Ceast Geard, expected in due time, should be very
helpful in determining further action to be taken by our Government.

=== Page 595 === (confidence 0.94)
- 3 -
It should be mentioned also that officials of the Department
of State, both in Washington and in Tokyo, have been keenly alert
to the Japanese fishery situation as it may affect Alaska, and
have handled the subject in a highly satisfactory manner.
Attached is copy of an extended article, entitled "Protect
our Alaska salmon from elien exploitation", appearing in the Sep-
tember, 1936, 1ssue of Pacific Fisherman, which reviews the situ-
ation in a broad and able way.
Acting Commissioner.
STBIPJH
moB
