=== Page 596 === (confidence 0.99)
Fom - Pacific Fisherman
September, 1936.
Page 21.
PROTECT OUR ALASKA SALMON PROM ALIEN EXPLOITATION
Japamse salmon canneries on Bristol Bay? A few years ago the idea
would have seemed utterly preposterous. In the light of recent events,
however, the next year or two, or at most three years, may be expected to
see Japanese floating canneries, with a great spread of gear, operating
close to the three-mile limit along the shores of Bristol Bay, offering
keen and devastating competition to American fishermen and packers, unless
vigorous steps are taken to prevent.
Last winter, as reported in these columns, Japanese fishing interests
pressed their government to issue licenses for salmon fishing and packing
in Bristol Bay waters - the world's greatest Red salmon area, hitherto
regarded as a distinotly American resource. Such licenses were refused
for the time being, but officials promised to investigate, "both politically
and scientifically," the problem of obtaining for their people a share in
this great fishery.
À little later Japan authorized and provided funds for a three-year
investigation of the fisheries of that region, with the evident view of
satisfying the demands of its people. A trawler and an auxiliary motor
vessel were assigned to the work, and severai fishery experts spent the
season studying conditions in Alaska fishing districts. While the investi-
gation is concerned partly with technological and operating conditions, the
basic objective is evidently to determine the practical commercial feasibility
of salmon fishing in extraterritorial waters off Alaska; and preliminary re-
sults are said to be highly satisfactory from the Japanese viewpoint.
Developments in the Orient
After the inception of salmon canning in Mamchatka, about l910, Japanese
interests quickly moved into the picture, operating under rights given them
through the Treaty of Portsmouth. Taking advantage of disorganised conditions
in Russia during and after the World War, they rapidly strengthened their
position and within a few years became the dominant factor in the fisheries
of that region; and their dominance has become more strongly fortified from
year to year.
This development was marked by a series of mergers through which, about
1932, all the Japanese shore operations in Kamchatka were brought into the
hands of one company, which is now the world's largest single salmon canning
organisation. But these shore operations were not enoughtt a notable feature
in more recent years has been the introduction of floating cannery enterprises,
accompanied by the development of deep-sea drift-net fishing on an enormous
and highly productive seale - the gear being operated both in conjunction
with floating canneries and with shore plants on the Kurile Islands.

=== Page 597 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 
These drift-nets, with rapidly increasing efficiency, intercepted
the runs and choked various passages followed by the salmon on their way
to important spawning streams - and incidentally to the inshore fishing
grounds used by the Kamchatka land canneries. The nets are said to be
operated by large sea-going boats, several hundred of which were in use
this year; the gear being made up of sections some 60 metres long, a
hundred of which are joined together to make ourtains of gillnet about
3-1/2 miles in length.
Several years ago these nets, spread out over a vast area, were
found to be obstructing the runs to Kamchatka and some other fishing and
spawning grounds, and seriously interfering with the catch of the Kam-
chatka shore canneries. Fears of resulting depletion were augmented by
the failure of last year's Red run and the Japanese government, becoming
alarmed for the future, sought to limit the number of drift-nets; but
this appears to have been very difficult for both economic and political
reasons. Japanese operators, faced with the prospect of depletion and
government restriction, are now looking for new fields of exploitation.
Rolation to Alaska
Until very recently all this has seemed of merely academic interest
to American operators, who have felt secure in the stability of their own
resources, meintained and built up by our government's wise conservation
policy. Even the appearance of Japanese floating crab canneries in the
Bristol Bay region failed to alarm us: we were not interested in deep-sea
crabs, and were told that the Orientals sought nothing else in that area.
Some further anxiety has been occasioned in the last few years by
the fact that these Japanese floaters were not confining their operations
to crab fishing, but were scraping the bottom with great dragnets for
cod, hake, halibut, etc., taking a heavy tonnage of groundfish for meal
and oil, freesing and salting. Even this did not worry us greatly; those
were high-seas fish, and apparently included but few of the more highly
prised varieties. Our salmon packers went blissfully ahead with their
own operations, gladly consenting to such restrictions as seemed neces-
sary to build up the salmon runs and giving little heed to other fishery
developments.
Mot until late last year, when it was learned that at least one of
the floaters had been canning salmon, was there any evident occasion
for alarm. A few, indeed, had foreseen possible danger of an Oriental
invasion of the Alaska salmon fisheries, but doubted the feasibility of
profitable operation of floating salmon canneries without shore connec-
tions in the Alaska region. It is now evident, however, that we face
the imminent prospect of Japanese floaters moving in on the Bristol Bay
salmon fishery, unless effective action is taken to stop it.

=== Page 598 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
No Serious Obstacles
Natural conditions offer no great obstacle. The crab canneries and
reduction plants for some years past have fully demonstrated the possi-
bility of operating outside the three-mile limit without touching shore:
the technique of large-scale high-seas gillnetting for salmon has been
developed in the Orient to a remarkable degree of efficiency. There is
no apparent reason why similar methods cannot be applied, with similar
results, off the shores of Bristol Bay. Experimental sets have already
given successful results, and it is possible that these methods may be
found applicable off other districts of Alaska as well.
Whother such ventures would be commercially profitable is yet to be
fully demonstrated. The cost, no doubt, would be relatively high as com-
pared with land canneries; but a comparison of the labor and other costs
on the Japanese floaters with those of the present Bristol Bay canneries,
taking into consideration their respective fishing methods, remuneration
of labor and fishermen, and other conditions, might easily show an advan-
tage on the side of the Orientals. Purther, aliens operating offshore would
be completely exempt from the handicap of American regulatory measures.
Aliens, of course, are not permitted to fish in the American waters of
Alaska. In the past that has been thought to afford sufficient protection,
but it seems altogether inadequate to cope with the new situation. For
gear of the type now permitted to Americans, the waters inshore and around
the river-mouths are, no doubt, the most productive and most frequented
fishing grounds - as was the case in Kamchatka before the deep-sea drift-
nets appeared. But these offshore nets introduce a new factor. They have
been highly successful in the open ocean off Kamchatka; they have shown
their ability to catch salmon in the open waters of Bristol Bay, and there
1s little doubt that they can catch them in at least substantial quantities
in that locality.
Effect Would Be Disastrous
Success of such undertakings would mean that Japanese interests would
be taking salmon bred in American streams and lakes, protected by the ef-
forts of the American government through the restricted operations of
American packers, to compete with the American product in the world's mar-
ket and perhaps even in our own home trade.
If these enormous Japanese nets succeed in intercepting the main
Bristol Bay run in offshore waters, they can take most of the fish before
they come within reach of the present American gear - reduce the catch of
American fishermen to negligible proportions, and the pack of American can-
neries to a fraction of their present output.
Worse than that, such operations, unrestrained by American conserva-
tion measures, would mean early and complete annihilation of the Bristol Bay
salmon - the greatest and most treasured of American fishery resouroes -
abandonment of canneries, impairment or destruction of capital investment
amounting to milliens - unemployment for thousands of American fishermen and
workers: and all this without hope of restoration. This would be a major
disaster.

=== Page 599 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
According to Japanese accounts, this high-seas gear is held largely
responsible for depletion of Red salmon runs in Kamchatka and northern
Japanese waters, which are regarded as in perilous condition. Can we ex-
peot it to be less destructive on this side? In the Orient, the Japanese
government has sought to restrict the activities of such gear because of
its interference with Japanese shore operations and impairment of Japanese
resources; but it has no such incentive to exercise restraint off the
Alaska coast.
Mast Take Vigorous Action
If the fishery is seriously invaded by aliens exempt from American
regulation, there would be no object in contimuing to hamper American pro-
ducers by the restrictions through which the resource has thus far been
preserved and built up. It would then be only fair and logical to cancel
all regulations, permit the industry to take all the fish it can by any
means it sees fit. That might result in excessive production for a few
years; it would inevitably mean a complete and permanent end of the fish-
ery, perhaps in five years - in ten years at most.
Some of the Japanese would probably like a proposition that this
fishery be shared with them on some specific terms, under which the opera-
tions of both nationalities might be so controlled as to assure permanence.
Such an arrangement, owever, would be obviously and grossly unfair to the
Americans, who have built up the industry and at whose expense the resource
has been brought to a point of stable productiveness - unfair to the
packers and their employees, the government, and the people at large.
Alaska salmon fishing regulations are based on the principle that the
catch will be so limited as not to exhaust the resource. The Bristol Bay
area produces an average pack of nearly a million and a quarter cases,
which is the maximum safe limit. Operation by the Japanese in extraterri-
torial waters would not mean added production, but taking from the produs-
tion hitherto enjoyed by Americans. Even if controlled, it would mean
sharp curtailment of American operations for the benefit of aliens, who
have contributed nothing to the resource, its development or its preserva-
tion; whese products are in direct competition with ours in the world
market, and who have no shadow of legitimate claim to any share in this
fishery.
Such submission to unfair impositions has never been characteristic
of Americans. Shall we set a new precedent by consenting to such demands?
Such a course is unworthy of serious consideration.
Only by a firm stand and vigorous action can we sustain our right and
be true to Ameriean traditions. Our government should promptly make diplo-
matic representations to Japen, to the end that that nation shall permanently
deny its nationals permission to operate in the American salmon fisheries.
In the great and manifold commercial relations between the two countries,
there are ample grounds on which Japan would find it advisable to forego a
profect'of this kind, which would inevitably be accompanied by serious

=== Page 600 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
difficulty and embarrassment, and the final results of which would be
of dubious value. Let no one forget that, in the tension of world
affairs, Japan needs friends - needs them badly - and cannot afford
unnecessarily to create antagonism.
Alaska Salmon Are American
Regardless of anything else, however, we can and must by all means
maintain and insist upon our inherent, exclusive and proprietary right to
these salmon, wherever they may be found, as a distinctive natural resource
of Alaska. Such a claim can be supported on substantial grounds, and is
based on recognised biological, geographical and economic facts.
As distinguished from the pelagic fishes which breed and live their
lives in the ccean, the Bristol Bay salmon are spawmed and hatched in the
inland waters of Alaska; live one or two years in those waters before going
to sea, and return there to spawn at the close of their cycle. Alaska is
their home: without it they could not exist. In their ocean travels, like
American ships, they may well be regarded as constituting in themselves a
bit of American territory.
But our claim is much stronger than this. Not only has the American
fishery been developed, but the salmon resource itself has been nurtured
and sustained through Americen effort and expense. It has, one might almost
say, been created by Americans, and is intrinsically theirs. he nature
and habits of the fish have been learned through years of intensive study
by American scientists; this knowledge has been applied to the preservation
of the species, and to restoring it when it had reached a state of advanced
depletion.
The salmon have been protected from extermination and restored to abun-
dance by depriving our fishermen and packers of the privilege of unlimited
fishing: without such limitation, the resource would long ago have lost
most of its value. It has been built up, also, by care and improvement of
the spawning areas at the direct expemse of the industry. Shall we now be
called upon to turn it over to aliens who have had no share in these efforts?
In view of the facts above mentioned, the position of the salmon is
similar to that of a litter of pigs or a flock of chickens, bred and cared
fer on a farmer's land and at his expense, which wander onto the public
demain - they do not by such wandering pass from his ownership, and cannot
rightly be appropriated by his neighber. Compared with other fishes, the
salmon have much in common with domestic animals as compared with wild game.
A Fundamental Principle
This discussion has concerned the Japanese, as they are the only aliens
mow showing am interest in the Bristol Bay salmon; but it would apply
equally te eperators of any other nationality. If the Japanese are per-
mitted te enter the Bristol Bay salmon fishery, the Russians, Canadians,
Mexicans er others would have equal right to do so.

=== Page 601 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
Further, if they can taks salmon in Bristol Bay, why not in other
parts of Alaska, or anywhere along the Coast? We limit our gear to certain
types, but methods may be devised by which salmon of any species can be
taken in many districts before the run enters territorial waters. Bristol
Bay is not the only district in jeopardy.
An agreement with Japan in this connection would not give us perma-
nent security; would not prevent boats of other nations from invading the
fishery, without control or limit. What is needed and must be obtained is
world-wide recognition of the fact that salmon bred and spawmed in United
States territory and protected by United States conservation measures, even
though they stray beyond territorial waters, still belong fundamentally to
this nation.
It is the duty of our government to proclaim and maintain this prin-
ciple; to demand its recognition; to devise end promulgate firm policies
that will effectively protect the American fisheries. Our right will be
recognised if we assert it and insist upon it - as Japan has repeatedly
and successfully insisted upon carrying out its national policies, regard-
less of opposition, even when its claim was based on no such firm ground
as is ours in Alaska. All that is needed is reasonable firmness and energy
in maintaining our right.
Stand Together For Defense
Our government has often been remiss in such duties. It is not easily
aroused to the importance, the necessity of resisting unwarranted impositions
on its remote frontiers. It has many other things on its hends, which seem
to it of greater importance, and Bristol Bay is a long way from Washington.
To arouse it to action, and obtain its support for the protection of our fish-
eries, will require the best ability, the determined, insistent effort and
cooperation of the entire industry.
And that means not only the packers, but allied trades, fishermen, labor-
ers and other employees as well - all Americans whose welfare depends in any
measure on the salmon fishery. The threat just now is to Bristol Bay - but
if this goes unchallenged, tomorrow it will be to other salmon areas; to the
entire Pacific Coast; to other fisheries than salmon, which heve hitherto
been regarded as distinctively American. This menace should meet the united
resistence of all branches of the industry.
Each fisherman would suffer more downright personal hardship from a
foreign invasion of these resources than would any packer. Fishermen and
workers have been demanding greater and greater remuneration; they are
getting and will get all that the industry can afford; but they need to
realise that there will be no pay and no jobs in the fishery if the fish
are destroyed or caught by aliens. Many other branches of labor also derive
benefit from this industry. The concerted effort of all will be needed if
we are to establish the principle that the American fisheries belong to
Americans.
